A perturbative approach to the Wetterich equation for Bosonic and Fermionic interacting fields
Beatrice Costeri

Abstract
We study the Lorentzian Wetterich Renormalization Group (RG) flow equation for interacting quantum fields on curved backgrounds within the framework of perturbative Algebraic Quantum Field Theory (pAQFT). Specifically, we consider two classes of models: two mutually interacting scalar fields on globally hyperbolic spacetimes without boundary and, under the further assumption that the underlying background is spin, self-interacting Dirac fields . In both cases, we derive the corresponding RG flow equations within a Local Potential Approximation and compute the beta functions for the relevant couplings. For the scalar model, we also discuss an asymmetric interaction potential which is formally reminiscent of the Martin-Siggia-Rose description of a stochastic dynamics, thereby indicating a possible connection between Lorentzian algebraic RG methods [ DDP+24 ] and stochastic field-theoretic models, [ Duc25 ] . In addition, we address the local well-posedness of the resulting flow equations. Adapting the strategy detailed in [ DP23 ] and based on the the Nash-Moser theorem, we prove local existence and uniqueness of solutions for both the scalar and the Dirac models. Keywords: Perturbative Algebraic Quantum Field Theory, Wetterich equation 2020 MSC classes: 81T05



1
Introduction

In this paper, we discuss the local well-posedness of Lorentzian Wetterich-type flow equations for interacting quantum fields on
d-
dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetimes with empty boundary. More precisely, we consider two classes of models: two mutually interacting scalar fields and self-interacting Dirac fields.

The natural framework for addressing these issues is
perturbative Algebraic Quantum Field Theory (pAQFT)
, which provides a local and covariant construction of quantum field theories on Lorentzian backgrounds, both in the free and in the interacting case,
cf.

[
BDF09
,
Rej16
]
. In this setting, one starts from a suitable algebra of classical observables, modelled as functionals on the configuration space of the theory, and then deforms its pointwise product into a non-commutative quantum product. For Bosonic fields, the deformation is governed by the causal propagator, namely, the retarded-minus-advanced fundamental solution of the operator ruling the dynamics, and it encodes the canonical commutation relations [CCR]. For Fermionic fields, the construction is performed in the corresponding graded setting: the deformation is induced by the causal fundamental solution of the Dirac operator, together with the spinorial pairing, and it encodes the canonical anticommutation relations [CAR]. States are then introduced as positive, normalized linear functionals on the quantum algebra. In applications, one usually restricts attention to quasi-free Hadamard states, whose two-point functions have a prescribed microlocal singular structure. This condition is essential in order to define physically relevant observables, such as the stress-energy tensor
[
Mor97
,
Mor03
,
CDG26
]
, both in the free theory and in interacting models. Interactions are incorporated perturbatively through the Bogoliubov map, which associates to a classical observable its interacting quantum counterpart as a formal power series in the coupling constants. This perturbative framework is particularly natural from the viewpoint of physical applications on Lorentzian spacetimes: it preserves locality and covariance, and, at the same time, it allows for an algorithmic control of the renormalization freedoms of the underlying theory. These features make pAQFT especially well suited for deriving Lorentzian Renormalization Group flow equations, where the rôle of the inverse propagator entering the Wetterich equation must be replaced by state-dependent, microlocally well-behaved propagators.

Renormalization Group (RG) flow equations
capture in a systematic way how the effective description of a quantum field theory changes with respect to a scale parameter. In Wilson’s approach to the Renormalization Group
[
WK74
,
FPS+25
]
, this flow encodes the progressive transition from microscopic degrees of freedom to macroscopic observables, yielding a coarse-grained picture of the system at low energies. In the functional formulation, the RG flow is implemented at the level of a scale-dependent effective average action
\Gamma_{k}
,
cf
.,
[
DCE+21
,
BTW02
]
. The latter interpolates between the microscopic action at the ultraviolet scale and the full quantum effective action in the infrared regime, and its evolution is governed, in the Euclidean setting, by the
Wetterich equation

[
Wet91
,
Wet93
]
. Although the Wetterich equation has the algebraic form of a one-loop trace, the propagator entering its right-hand side is the full scale-dependent propagator, namely the inverse of
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}+Q_{k}
, where
Q_{k}
is an infrared regulator, rather than a fixed free propagator. The equation is therefore non-linear in the average effective action
\Gamma_{k}
and encodes non-perturbative information. This feature makes the Functional Renormalization Group particularly suited to approximation schemes such as the derivative expansion and the
Local Potential Approximation

[
DP23
,
DDP+24
]
, while at the same time allowing for the dynamical generation of interaction terms along the flow
[
Mor94
,
BTW02
]
. In Lorentzian signature, however, the formulation of RG flow equations requires additional care, since the hyperbolic operator admits canonical retarded and advanced Green’s operators, but no distinguished Feynman inverse or vacuum two-point function. Furthermore, the infrared regulator
Q_{k}
has to be compatible with covariance, causality and the microlocal structure of quantum states.

In this paper, following the algebraic approach to quantum field theory
[
BDF09
]
and the Lorentzian formulation of Wetterich-type equations developed in
[
DP23
,
DDP+24
]
, we derive RG flow equations for two classes of interacting theories: two mutually interacting scalar fields on globally hyperbolic, Lorentzian spacetimes
({\mathcal{M}},g_{{\mathcal{M}}})
without boundary and – under additional requirements on the underlying spin structure of
{\mathcal{M}}
– self-interacting Dirac fields. In both scenarios, the resulting flow governs the scale dependence of the corresponding effective interaction, and, within the chosen approximation scheme, yields the beta functions for the relevant couplings. The beta functions obtained in this way are consistent with recent results in the physics literature
[
JWK+21
,
BS25
]
.
Beyond the derivation of the beta functions, we also address the local well-posedness of the resulting RG flow equations: adapting the strategy of
[
DP23
]
, we prove the existence and uniqueness of local solutions in both cases by means of the Nash-Moser theorem,
cf.
,
[
Mos66a
,
Mos66b
,
Nas56
,
Ham82
]
.

As an application of the results derived in this work, we succinctly discuss a model of two mutually interacting scalar fields via a potential asymmetric in the fields. At the formal level, this model closely resembles the field-theoretic description of stochastic dynamics obtained through the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism
[
BDD25
]
, although it is here considered in a Lorentzian setting. This passage is delicate. Indeed, stochastic equations driven by additive Gaussian white noise are naturally formulated in Euclidean or parabolic frameworks, where the relevant elliptic or parabolic operator selects, under suitable assumptions, a distinguished Green kernel. By contrast, in Lorentzian signature the underlying wave operator admits no unique inverse: retarded, advanced and Feynman-type inverses encode different causal or state-dependent prescriptions. This lack of a preferred inverse is precisely one of the structural reasons why the Lorentzian formulation of RG flow equations requires the tools of perturbative algebraic quantum field theory, where the choice of a Hadamard state and the associated microlocal control replace the Euclidean momentum-space construction. Conversely, even in the Euclidean algebraic approach, the construction of Wick powers, Euclidean products and interacting observables is not automatic, but relies on suitable parametrices and on the control of the associated renormalization ambiguities, as discussed in
[
DDR20
]
.

Singular Euclidean stochastic scalar field theories have recently been analysed via complementary methods, notably paracontrolled calculus and related flow-equation techniques
[
GIP15
,
CC18
,
GKO24
,
Duc25
,
DGR23
]
. In particular, in the flow-equation approach one introduces scale-dependent effective force coefficients, whose relevant components play a rôle analogous to running couplings in the functional RG picture. However, the precise relation between this stochastic, Polchinski-type description and the Wetterich-type equations obtained within the Lorentzian pAQFT framework remains unclear. In this sense, the possible correspondence between perturbative, algebraic and non-perturbative RG descriptions of stochastic field theories is still largely unexplored.

A further natural direction, not pursued here, is the extension of the present analysis to spacetimes with timelike boundaries. In this setting, boundary conditions affect both the causal propagators and the microlocal structure of admissible states. The analysis of the Lorentzian Wetterich equation in the presence of Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions in
[
DNS25
]
, together with the construction of Hadamard states for Robin boundary conditions in
[
CDJ+26
]
, provides a natural starting point for understanding the interplay between boundary conditions and RG flow. This would clarify whether the corresponding Wetterich-type equation admits a local and covariant interpretation compatible with the renormalization freedoms already present in the bulk theory.

Synopsis –

The outline of the paper is the following.

In Section
2
, we review key notions of perturbative algebraic quantum field theory (pAQFT), first in the general setting of arbitrary vector bundles and then specialized to the cases under consideration. Section
2.1
is devoted to the construction of both the free and the covariant quantum algebra of local observables for two mutually interacting scalar fields on globally hyperbolic spacetimes without boundary. In addition, in Section
2.1
, we derive the Renormalization Group flow equations within the Local Potential Approximation framework from the Wetterich equation. As a relevant application of the formalism developed in this section, we consider an asymmetric interaction potential reminiscent of stochastic fields in the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism,
cf
., Section
3.1.1
.
Section
2.2
is devoted to the analysis of the algebraic framework for self-interacting Dirac fields, where anticommutativity is encoded in the properties of the deformation map. In this setting, we also derive the Wetterich equation and determine the scaling behaviour of the relevant couplings in three dimensions,
cf.
, Section
2.2
.

In Section
3
, we establish the local existence of solutions to the Wetterich equation via the Nash-Moser Theorem
3.7
, both for two mutually interacting scalar fields,
cf.
, Section
3.1
and for Dirac fields,
cf.
, Section
3.2
.

Appendix
A
collects the necessary background material for the application of the Nash-Moser theorem to the Renormalization Group flow equations,
e.g.
, the notion of tame maps between graded Fréchet spaces, see Definition
A.2
.

2
A pAQFT approach to flow equations for interacting fields

The aim of this section is to derive the Lorentzian Wetterich-type RG equation for two quantum field theoretic models: two mutually interacting scalar fields and self-interacting Dirac fields, discussed respectively in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
. To this avail, we first fix the geometric setting and recall the general structures underlying perturbative Algebraic Quantum Field Theory (pAQFT),
[
BDF09
,
BDF+15
,
Rej16
]
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}},g_{\mathcal{M}})
be a
d-
dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime,
d\geq 2
, with
g
a Lorentzian metric of signature
(1,d-1)
,
cf
.,
[
HE73
]
. Consider thereon some finite-rank vector bundle
E\equiv E[{\mathcal{M}},\pi,V]
, where the fibre
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space,
i.e.
,
\text{dim}(V)=n<\infty
. We denote by
\mathcal{E}(E):=\Gamma^{\infty}(E)
the
configuration space
of the field theory under scrutiny, corresponding to the space
C^{\infty}(E\leftarrow{\mathcal{M}})
of smooth sections of the bundle
E
.

Example 2.1
.

We now present some illustrative examples of configuration spaces.

(i)

Consider
n\in\mathbb{N}
real or complex scalar fields on
({\mathcal{M}},g_{\mathcal{M}})
. In this case,
E\equiv E[{\mathcal{M}},\pi,\mathbb{K}^{n}]
is the trivial
\mathbb{K}
-vector bundle, where
\mathbb{K}
denotes either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, over
{\mathcal{M}}
,
i.e.
,
E
is globally diffeomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Smooth sections of this bundle
\mathcal{E}(E):=\Gamma^{\infty}(E)\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{K}^{n}
correspond to
field multiplets
, where
\otimes
denotes the algebraic tensor product. In particular, for a real scalar field,
E\simeq{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R}
. In this case, the kinematical field configurations are identified with smooth, real-valued functions on
{\mathcal{M}}
, that is,
\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}}):=\Gamma^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}},\mathbb{R})
. The case of two mutually interacting scalar fields is discussed in Section
2.1
.

(ii)

Under the additional assumptions that
{\mathcal{M}}
is spin with a trivial spin bundle
S{\mathcal{M}}
, the rôle of
E
is played the Dirac bundle
D{\mathcal{M}}=S{\mathcal{M}}\times_{\sigma_{d}}\Sigma_{d}
, where
\Sigma_{d}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}
is the
spinor space
, whilst
\sigma_{d}:\text{Spin}(d)\rightarrow GL(\Sigma_{d})
is the
spinor representation
– see . Spinor fields are smooth sections of the Dirac bundle,
i.e.
,
\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}},\Sigma_{d})
– see Section
2.2
for additional details.

(iii)

Let
\mathrm{G}
be some compact Lie group and denote by
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}})
the corresponding Lie algebra. For Yang-Mills theories with a trivial principal bundle
\mathrm{P}\simeq\mathcal{M}\times\mathrm{G}
, the adjoint bundle
\text{Ad}(\mathrm{P})\simeq{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathfrak{g}
plays the rôle of the vector bundle
E
. In this setting, the space of field configurations coincides with the space of
\mathfrak{g}-
valued
1-
forms on
{\mathcal{M}}
,
i.e.
,
\mathcal{E}(\text{Ad}(\mathrm{P})):=\Omega^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\text{Ad}(\mathrm{P}))\simeq\Omega^{1}({\mathcal{M}},\mathfrak{g})
– see
[
Rej16
, Ch. 7]
.

The configuration space
\mathcal{E}(E)
can be equipped with a Fréchet topology induced by a suitable family of seminorms. Since
\mathcal{M}
is a smooth, second-countable Hausdorff manifold, it is paracompact and, hence, it admits a Riemannian metric
g_{R}
. On the vector bundle
E
, we fix a fibre metric
h
and a compatible connection
\nabla^{E}
. Let
\{K_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
be an exhaustion of
\mathcal{M}
by compact sets. For
j,m\in\mathbb{N}
, define

p_{j,m}(s):=\sup_{x\in K_{m}}\|\nabla^{j}s(x)\|_{g_{R},h},\quad s\in\Gamma^{\infty}(E),

(2.1)

where the norm is induced by
g_{R}
and
h
. The seminorms
\{p_{j,m}\}_{j,m}
generate a locally convex topology
\tau
on
\mathcal{E}(E)
, with respect to which
\Gamma^{\infty}(E)
is a nuclear Fréchet space, see,
e.g.
,
[
Tre67
, Ch. 10]
, in particular Example I for the analysis of the trivial bundle case.

In this context, physically relevant observables can be modelled – both at a classical and quantum level – as smooth functionals from the configuration space
\mathcal{E}(E)
of the underlying theory to the complex field
\mathbb{C}
. The reason for considering complex-valued objects, as opposed to real-valued ones, lies in the fact that the quantization procedure, even for real field theories, inherently involves the introduction of a complex structure.
Henceforth, we shall confine our attention to those complex-valued functionals
F:\mathcal{E}(E)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, which are smooth in the Bastiani sense,
cf.
,
[
Bas64
]
,
[
Ham82
]
,
[
Mil84
]
,
[
Nee06
]
. In particular, to define the functional derivatives of such
F
, we shall adopt the Michal-Bastiani notion of differentiability, see
[
Mic38
]
and
[
Mic84
]
. Following slavishly
[
Rej16
, Ch. 3]
, we denote by

\mathcal{F}(E):=\{F:\mathcal{E}(E)\to\mathbb{C}\,|\,F\,\text{is Bastiani smooth in the sense of \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rejzner}{}{}, Def. 3.4]}}\},

(2.2)

the
space of functionals
of the underlying field theory.

First and foremost, we need to equip the space
\mathcal{F}(E)
with a suitable notion of
localisation
of functionals on the spacetime manifold
\mathcal{M}
. The underlying motivation is that physical observables are intrinsically local. Therefore, in the framework of algebraic quantum field theory, one aims to associate algebraic structures to bounded regions of spacetime in a way compatible with causality,
cf.
,
[
BDF+15
]
,
[
Haa96
]
,
[
Ara99
]
. In this regard, it is essential to distinguish functionals according to the region of spacetime on which they effectively depend. This allows one to determine whether a given observable is localized in a prescribed region and, consequently, to organize
\mathcal{F}(E)
into a net of local algebras
\mathcal{O}\mapsto\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{O})
indexed by suitable spacetime domains
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{M}
. From a functional analytic viewpoint, the localisation properties of functionals also play a crucial rôle in ensuring that algebraic operations remain compatible with the microlocal regularity conditions required for their well-definiteness, see
[
Hör90
]
,
[
BFK96
]
,
[
Rej16
]
. To this end, we introduce the notion of
spacetime support
of functionals.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}(E)
be as per Equation (
2.2
). We call
spacetime support
of
F
the set

\text{supp}(F):=\{x\in{\mathcal{M}}\,|\,\forall\mathcal{O}_{x},\exists\eta,\theta\in\mathcal{E}(E),\text{supp}(\theta)\subset\mathcal{O}_{x}\,\text{such that}\,F(\eta+\theta)\neq F(\eta)\},

(2.3)

where
\mathcal{O}_{x}\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is a neighbourhood of
x
in
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Notice that the above definition admits a natural generalisation to Fréchet derivatives of functionals, which can be regarded as elements of
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}^{k},(E^{*})^{\boxtimes k})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where
E^{*}
denotes the dual bundle to
E
– see also Definition
2.12
.

To avoid unnecessary complications, from now on we abandon the general bundle setting and focus instead on the concrete models considered in this work: two mutually interacting scalar fields, see Section
2.1
, and self-interacting Dirac fields,
cf.
, Section
2.2
. This restriction will be in place for both the construction of the free and interacting algebras of observables. A more thorough treatment of the general framework can be found in
[
Rej16
]
. The same distinction will be retained in the derivation of the corresponding Lorentzian Wetterich-type equations in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
, in the spirit of the construction developed in
[
DP23
]
.

2.1
(A) Two mutually interacting scalar fields

On top of
({\mathcal{M}},g_{\mathcal{M}})
, consider two real, mutually interacting, scalar fields
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R})
. The full action of the theory is thus specified by

I[\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}]:=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left(\frac{1}{2}\left[\sum_{\ell=1,2}g^{\mu\nu}(x)\partial_{\mu}\varphi_{\ell}(x)\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{\ell}(x)+(m_{\ell}^{2}+\xi_{\ell}R)\varphi_{\ell}^{2}(x)\right]+V(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})(x)\right)\,f(x),

(2.4)

where
d\mu_{x}
denotes the metric-induced volume measure on
{\mathcal{M}}
and
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}})
. In addition, we assume that the two fields have different masses, namely
m_{1}\neq m_{2}
. In Equation (
2.4
), the functional
V(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
is assumed to have a polynomial dependence in the fields. In particular, we shall consider a symmetric interacting potential of the form

V_{h}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[\frac{\lambda_{1}}{4!}\varphi^{4}_{1}(x)+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4!}\varphi^{4}_{2}(x)+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{4}\varphi_{1}^{2}(x)\varphi_{2}^{2}(x)\right]h(x),\quad\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0,\,\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}>9\lambda_{3}^{2}.

(2.5)

where
h\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}})
is an adiabatic cut-off needed to avoid infrared divergences. The assumptions
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
and

\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}>9\lambda_{3}^{2}
ensure that the quartic part of the
potential in Equation (
2.5
) is bounded from
below. Indeed, setting
\rho_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{1}^{2}
and

\rho_{2}:=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{2}^{2}
, and disregarding the smooth cut-off, the integrand
can be written as

V(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})(x)=\frac{1}{3!}\left(\lambda_{1}\rho_{1}^{2}(x)+6\lambda_{3}\rho_{1}(x)\rho_{2}(x)+\lambda_{2}\rho_{2}^{2}(x)\right).

The quadratic form in the variables
(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
has an associated matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}&3\lambda_{3}\\
3\lambda_{3}&\lambda_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

The stated assumptions are precisely the conditions ensuring that this matrix
is positive-definite.

Remark 2.4
.

We stress that the splitting of the total action as the sum of free and interacting contributions in Equation (
2.4
) carries an intrinsic degree of arbitrariness. Indeed, another admissible choice would be to include into the interacting potential also the mass terms, which are quadratic in the fields. Yet, on account of the principle of perturbative agreement – see
[
DHP17
]
, such a choice does not affect the final result.

Remark 2.5
.

Note that the two scalar fields
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})
can equivalently be regarded as a single smooth section
\Phi\in\Gamma^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R}^{2})
of the trivial vector bundle
{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Remark 2.6
.

In Equation (
2.4
) we have implicitly assumed that the two fields are coupled only by the interaction term. Working in full generality, we could also have incorporated into the theory a mass term of the form
m_{3}^{2}\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}
. Indeed, being such a contribution of zeroth order, it does not affect the Green hyperbolicity of the Klein-Gordon operator. Yet, dealing with this term at the level of the free dynamics yields several technical complications, as it prevents the decoupling of the free equations of motion – see Equation (
2.15
). To bypass this hurdle, we can invoke the
principle of perturbative agreement

[
DHP17
]
to include it in the interacting potential. Nonetheless, as will be shown in detail in Section
2.1
, the presence of such a mixed term precludes the choice of a diagonal infrared regulator and, hence, it further complicates the derivation of the Wetterich equation.

As a preliminary step towards the construction of a suitable algebra of observables for the theory described by Equation (
2.4
), we begin by fixing notations and conventions once and for all. Let
\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}}):=C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R})
. We denote by

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{M}):=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{M})\oplus\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{M}),

the
configuration space
of the theory, which is a nuclear, Fréchet space equipped with the product topology,
i.e.
, the Fréchet topology generated by the family of seminorms:

p_{n,K}^{(2)}(\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}):=\max\{p_{n,K}(\varphi)+p_{n,K}(\varphi^{\prime})\},\quad\text{for any compact set}\,K\subset{\mathcal{M}}\,\text{and for}\,\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}}),

where

p_{n,K}(\varphi):=\sup_{x\in K}\max_{m\leq n}|\varphi^{(n)}(x)|.

We can now introduce the key objects of our analysis.

Definition 2.7

(Space of functionals)
.

In the aforementioned setting, we call
space of functionals
of the quantum field theory described by Equation (
2.4
), the space

\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M}):=\{F:\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\,|\,F\,\text{is smooth in the Bastiani sense}\,\},

(2.6)

see
[
Rej16
]
.

Remark 2.8
.

An element
u\in\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
should actually be regarded a functional

F:\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})\times\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad(f;\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mapsto F(f;\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}),

which is linear in the first entry and smooth with respect to the locally convex topology of
\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
. Henceforth, with a slight abuse of notation, the dependence on the smooth, compactly supported test function will be left implicit and we shall still denote by
F
the functional
F_{f}:=F(f;\cdot,\cdot)
.

The space of functionals
\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
can be also endowed with a notion of Fréchet derivative as clarified by the following definition.

Definition 2.9

(Fréchet derivatives)
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
, where
\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
is the space of functionals as per Definition
2.7
. We call
Fréchet derivative
of
F
of order
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the functional-valued distribution
F^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}^{k_{1}+k_{2}};\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}}))
defined by

\displaystyle F^{(k_{1},k_{2})}:\underbrace{\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})}_{k_{1}-\text{times}}\otimes\underbrace{\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})}_{k_{2}-\text{times}}\times\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},

\displaystyle F(\eta_{1}\otimes

\displaystyle\ldots\otimes\eta_{k_{1}}\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\eta^{\prime}_{k_{2}};\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{k_{1}+k_{2}}}{\partial s_{1}\ldots\partial s_{k_{1}}\partial s^{\prime}_{1}\ldots\partial s^{\prime}_{k_{2}}}F(\varphi_{1}+s_{1}\eta_{1}+\ldots+s_{k_{1}}\eta_{k_{1}};\varphi_{2}+s^{\prime}_{1}\eta^{\prime}_{1}+\ldots+s^{\prime}_{k_{2}}\eta^{\prime}_{k_{2}})\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}=\ldots=s_{k_{1}}=0\\
s^{\prime}_{1}=\ldots=s^{\prime}_{k_{2}}=0\end{subarray}}.

(2.7)

Remark 2.10

(Polynomial functionals)
.

If a functional
F\in\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
has only a finite number of non-vanishing Fréchet derivatives,
F
is said to be of
polynomial
type. The space of polynomial functionals over
{\mathcal{M}}
will be denoted by
\text{Pol}({\mathcal{M}}).

Example 2.11
.

Notable examples of polynomial functionals which will play a crucial rôle in the following are:

\displaystyle\bm{1}[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,f(x),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\varphi_{1}(x)f(x),\quad\Phi_{2}[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\varphi_{2}(x)f(x),

(2.9)

where
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{M})
. A direct computation entails that

(\Phi_{1})^{(1,0)}[f](\eta_{1};\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\eta_{1}(x)f(x),\quad(\Phi_{2})^{(0,1)}[f](\eta_{2};\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\eta_{2}(x)f(x),

all higher functional derivatives being identically vanishing.
Particularly relevant in view of the ensuing discussion are also the functionals quadratic in the fields,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1}^{2})[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\varphi_{1}^{2}(x)f(x),

(2.10)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{2}^{2})[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\varphi_{2}^{2}(x)f(x),

(2.11)

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\equiv(\Phi_{2}\Phi_{1})[f](\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\varphi_{1}(x)\varphi_{2}(x)f(x).

(2.12)

Observe that we can endow
\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
with an algebra structure by defining thereon a notion of
pointwise product
: for any
F,G\in\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
and for any pair of field configurations
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})
,

(F\cdot G)(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=\iota^{*}[F(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\otimes G(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})],

(2.13)

where
\iota:\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\times\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}}),(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\mapsto((\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}),(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}))
is the diagonal map on the configuration space.

At the present stage, it would be tempting to consider as the classical
*
-algebra of observables the algebra of functionals on
{\mathcal{M}}
, equipped with the pointwise product as per Equation (
2.13
). At this level, there is no actual obstruction in making this choice. Yet, recall that in the algebraic formalism, the quantum information carried by the statistical correlations functions is encoded in the classical algebraic framework via a deformation of the algebra product. More precisely, this leads to considering pointwise products among derivatives of functionals lying in
\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})
and suitable distributional kernels with a non-trivial singular structure. On account of
[
Hör90
, Thm. 8.2.10]
, such products are
a priori
not necessarily well-defined. This issue can be avoided by focusing on a notable subclass of functionals possessing the sought-after microlocal behaviour.

Definition 2.12

(Microcausal functionals)
.

Let
k:=k_{1}+k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. We call
microcausal functionals
the elements of

\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{F\in\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,\text{WF}(F^{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}))\subseteq G_{k}({\mathcal{M}}),\forall k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\},

(2.14)

where

G_{k_{1}+k_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}):=T^{*}(\underbrace{{\mathcal{M}}\times\ldots\times{\mathcal{M}}}_{k-\text{times}}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{x\in{\mathcal{M}}}(V^{+}_{x})^{k}\cup\bigcup_{x\in{\mathcal{M}}}(V^{-}_{x})^{k}\right)).

In particular, we shall denote the space of polynomial microcausal functionals by
\text{Pol}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}}):=\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})\cap\text{Pol}({\mathcal{M}})
.

Furthermore, we can identify two notable subclasses of microcausal functionals:

❇

Regular functionals
:

\mathcal{F}_{reg}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,F^{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}),\forall k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

In particular, we denote by
\text{Pol}_{reg}({\mathcal{M}}):=\text{Pol}({\mathcal{M}})\cap\mathcal{F}_{reg}({\mathcal{M}})
.

❇

Local functionals
:

\mathcal{F}_{loc}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,\text{supp}(F^{(k_{1},k_{2})}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}))\in\text{Diag}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}({\mathcal{M}}),\forall k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\},

where
\text{supp}(F):=\{x\in{\mathcal{M}}\,|\,\forall U\in\mathcal{N}_{x},\exists\eta,\theta\in\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}}):\text{supp}(\eta)\subset U,F(\eta+\theta)\neq F(\theta)\}
,
\mathcal{N}_{x}
being a collection of open neighbourhoods of
x
, is the spacetime support of
F
, while
\text{Diag}_{k}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{(x,\ldots,x)\in{\mathcal{M}}^{k}\,|\,x\in{\mathcal{M}}\}
is the total diagonal of
{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
. The space of local functionals which are in addition polynomials in the fields is denoted by
\text{Pol}_{reg}({\mathcal{M}}):=\text{Pol}({\mathcal{M}})\cap\mathcal{F}_{loc}({\mathcal{M}}).

Example 2.13
.

Notable examples of polynomial local functionals are
\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f]
for
\ell=1,2
and the pointwise product
(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f]
, as one can check by direct inspection – see Example
2.11
. In general, all physically relevant observables – such as the stress-energy tensor of the underlying theory – belong to this class.

We now possess all the ingredients to characterise the classical algebra of observables. Heuristically speaking, since the interaction term is per assumption polynomial in the fields, such an algebra should contain any linear combinations of functionals with the desired microlocal behaviour, which are additionally of polynomial type as per Remark
2.10
.

Definition 2.14

(Classical microcausal algebra)
.

Let
({\mathcal{M}},g_{\mathcal{M}})
be a
d-
dimensional globally hyperbolic, Lorentzian spacetime with
d\geq 2
. We call
classical microcausal algebra
over
{\mathcal{M}}
, and we denote it by
\mathcal{A}_{cl}
, the commutative, unital
*-
algebra of microcausal functionals
\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})
, endowed with the pointwise product in Equation (
2.13
) and with an involution given by the complex conjugation.

To encompass into the classical framework the information of the correlation functions, we shall introduce a suitable deformation of the algebra structure. This procedure is known as
deformation quantization
and it consists in deforming the algebra product by means of a suitable bi-distribution. To identify the bi-distribution which will play this rôle, it is convenient to consider the dynamics of the free fields:

\begin{cases}P_{0,1}\varphi_{1}:=(-\Box_{g}+m_{1}^{2}+\xi_{1}R)\varphi_{1}=0\\
P_{0,2}\varphi_{2}:=(-\Box_{g}+m_{2}^{2}+\xi_{2}R)\varphi_{2}=0.\end{cases}

(2.15)

Observe that, in view of Remark
2.6
, the free dynamics is described by a system of uncoupled Green hyperbolic equations. Hence, there exists unique advanced
(A)
and retarded
(R)
fundamental solutions for the Klein-Gordon operators
P_{0,\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, which we shall denote by
\Delta_{A/R,\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
, respectively. Moreover, for each
\ell=1,2
, let
\Delta_{+,\ell}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}})
be the associated (global) Hadamard two-point functions. As a by-product of the factorization of the free field dynamics, no mixing between the two fields can occur. More precisely, in view of Remark
2.5
, we can write the correlations of the theory as a diagonal matrix-valued bi-distribution

\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}:=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{+,1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{+,2}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})).

(2.16)

Hence, in considering a suitable deformation of the pointwise product, a natural choice for the deforming bi-distributions is represented by the Hadamard two-point functions
\Delta_{\ell,+}
,
\ell=1,2
. For any
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})
and for each
\ell=1,2
, we define the
deformation maps
as

D_{\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell}}:=\langle\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell},\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}\rangle=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell}(x,y)\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}(x)}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}(y)},

and we introduce the
deformed product
acting as follows:

(F\star G)(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=\mathsf{M}\circ e^{\sum_{\ell=1,2}D_{\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell}}}\left[F(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\otimes G(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\right],

(2.17)

for any pair of field configurations
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
, where
\mathsf{M}
is the pull back on the space
\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}({\mathcal{M}})
via the diagonal map
\iota:\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\times\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
,
\iota(\Phi)=(\Phi,\Phi)
,
\Phi
denoting the field doublet as per Remark
2.5
.

Remark 2.15
.

Notice that, in Equation (
2.17
), we could have equivalently written the deformation map as

\sum_{\ell=1,2}D_{\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell}}:=D_{\hbar\,\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}},

(2.18)

where
\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}(\mathbf{\Delta}_{+})
denotes the trace taken with respect to the matrix indices of
\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}
– see Equation (
2.16
).

Equation (
2.17
) can also be recast in the form of a formal power series in
\hbar
:

F\star G:=F\cdot G+\sum_{k:=k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{\hbar^{k}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\langle F^{(k_{1},k_{2})},\Delta_{+,1}^{\otimes k_{1}}\Delta_{+,2}^{\otimes k_{2}}G^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\rangle,

where we have left the dependence on the test function and on the field configurations implicit for notational ease.

Remark 2.16
.

The choice of a deformation map constructed from the matrix trace of
\bm{\Delta}_{+}
is motivated by the desire to contract only fields of the same type. This reflects the physical requirement that correlations arise between fields of the same species, while at the free level they remain independent and are coupled only through the interaction.

We are now in the position to define the quantum counterpart of the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{cl}
.

Definition 2.17

(Quantum algebra of microcausal functionals)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
be as per Equation (
2.16
), where
\Delta_{+,\ell},\ell=1,2
are the Hadamard two-point functions of the underlying scalar theory. We call
quantum algebra of microcausal (polynomial) functionals
the unital, commutative
*-
algebra
(\text{Pol}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}({\mathcal{M}}),\star,\ast)
.

Remark 2.18
.

We recall that in this setting the expectation value of any observable
A\in\text{Pol}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}
is codified by the evaluation at vanishing fields, that is,
\langle A\rangle=A|_{\Phi=0}
.

Definition 2.19

(Local and covariant algebra)
.

We call
local and covariant algebra of microcausal polynomial functionals
the algebraic closure of
\text{Pol}_{loc}({\mathcal{M}})
under the product
\star_{H}
, where the deformation is implemented by
\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\bm{H}
with
\bm{H}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
such that
\bm{\Delta}_{+}-\bm{H}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
.

Additionally, we define the abstract Wick ordering of an observable
A\in\text{Pol}({\mathcal{M}})
as

:A:_{\bm{H}}:=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\langle\bm{H},\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\Phi(x)\delta\Phi(y)}\rangle}A=:\alpha_{-\bm{H}}(A),

(2.19)

where the pairing
\langle\cdot\rangle
is here defined on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
and the trace is taken with respect to the
\mathbb{C}^{2}-
indices. The following definition clarifies how to relate this construction to the concrete Wick ordering commonly employed in quantum field theory.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathcal{M})
be as per Definition
2.19
. Given a (global) Hadamard state
\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M})
as in Equation (
2.16
), we call operator ordering map of
\mathrm{Pol}(\mathcal{M})
in
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(\mathcal{M})
the application

\alpha_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}:\mathrm{Pol}(\mathcal{M})\to\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(\mathcal{M})

where
\alpha_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}
is as per Equation (
2.19
) with
\bm{H}
replaced by
\bm{\Delta}_{+}
. This is a
*
-isomorphism, namely, for all
F,G\in\mathrm{Pol}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(\mathcal{M})
,

F\star G=\alpha_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(\alpha^{-1}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(F)\,\alpha^{-1}_{\mathbf{\Delta}_{+}}(G)).

(2.20)

Example 2.21
.

In order to better clarify the above discussion, it is convenient to discuss an explicit computation. More precisely, we shall consider the deformed product of the quadratic functionals discussed in Example
2.11
. For any
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
and for all pair of field configurations
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})
, we have

\displaystyle(\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f]\star\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\varphi_{\ell}^{2}(x)\varphi_{\ell}^{2}(y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle+4\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta_{+,\ell}(x,y)\varphi_{\ell}(x)\varphi_{\ell}(y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle+2\hbar^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta^{2}_{+,\ell}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y),\quad\ell=1,2,

or, equivalently,

(\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f]\star\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=(\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f]\Phi_{\ell}^{2}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})+4\hbar\Delta_{+,\ell}(f,f^{\prime})(\Phi_{\ell}[f]\Phi_{\ell}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})+2\hbar^{2}\Delta_{+,\ell}^{2}(f,f^{\prime}).

Similarly, for the deformed product of the mixed quadratic functionals, we obtain

\displaystyle((\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f]\star(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\varphi_{1}(x)\varphi_{2}(x)\varphi_{1}(y)\varphi_{2}(y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle+\hbar\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell,j=1,2\\
\ell\neq j\end{subarray}}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta_{+,\ell}(x,y)\varphi_{j}(x)\varphi_{j}(y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle+\hbar^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta_{+,1}(x,y)\Delta_{+,2}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y),

and, in a more compact notation,

\displaystyle((\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f]\star(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=((\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f](\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle+\hbar\sum_{\ell\neq j}\Delta_{+,\ell}(f,f^{\prime})(\Phi_{j}[f]\Phi_{j}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})+\hbar^{2}\Delta_{+,1}(f,f^{\prime})\Delta_{+,2}(f,f^{\prime}).

Note that, upon evaluating the above expressions at vanishing field configurations, they yield the correlations of the underlying theory. Furthermore, to make contact with the standard notation adopted in quantum field theory, a pictorial representation can be employed:

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{2}[f]\star\Phi_{1}^{2}[f^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\Phi_{1}^{2}[f]\Phi_{1}^{2}[f^{\prime}]+4\hbar\hbox to7.22pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.61111pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

where each contraction highlighted in black is performed by means of
\Delta_{+,1}
, whilst red contractions are implemented by
\Delta_{+,2}
.

Interacting theory

We now possess all the ingredients to tackle the interacting scenario. Let us consider the potential in Equation (
2.5
), which depends only on polynomials in the fields. This requirement will be crucial in Section
3
, as it ensures that one can choose a local potential independent of the field derivatives, thereby preserving the Green hyperbolicity of the operator under scrutiny.
As for the case of a single, self-interacting scalar field, we can define the time ordered product via the Feynman propagators of the free theory. More precisely, for each
\ell=1,2
, we denote by
\Delta_{F,\ell}:=\Delta_{+,\ell}+i\Delta_{A,\ell}
the Feynman propagators relative to the fields
\varphi_{\ell}
. Also in this case, we can adopt a convenient matrix notation: let
\bm{\Delta}_{F}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})
be defined by

\bm{\Delta}_{F}(x,y):=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{F,1}(x,y)&0\\
0&\Delta_{F,2}(x,y)\end{pmatrix},

at the level of integral kernels. The time ordered product can then be completely characterised by the
time ordering map

\mathcal{T}^{\hbar\bm{H}_{F}}
acting on tensor products of elements in
\mathrm{Pol}_{loc}(\mathcal{M})
as per Definition
2.19
. Without delving into the axiomatic construction of such a product, we refer the reader to
[
BDF+15
]
and
[
Rej16
]
for further details on the topic. Here we limit ourselves to concretely define its action on the algebra
\mathrm{Pol}_{\bm{\Delta}_{+}}(\mathcal{M})
. The time ordering map can be explicitly realised exploiting the identity:

\mathcal{T}^{\hbar\bm{H}_{F}}(F_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes F_{n}):=F_{1}\star_{F}\ldots\star_{F}F_{n}=\mathsf{M}\circ e^{\sum_{k<j}^{n}D^{kj}_{\hbar\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\bm{H}_{F}}}(F_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes F_{n}),

(2.21)

where
\mathsf{M}
denotes the pull back on
\textrm{Pol}_{\mu c}(\mathcal{M})\otimes\textrm{Pol}_{\mu c}(\mathcal{M})
via the diagonal map
\iota
, while
F_{i}\in\mathrm{Pol}_{\mu c}(\mathcal{M})
, for all
i=1,\dots,n
. Here
\bm{H}_{F}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
denotes the Feynman parametrix, whilst

D^{kj}_{\hbar\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\bm{H}_{F}}:=\sum_{\ell=1,2}\langle\hbar H_{F,\ell},\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}\rangle,

which is manifestly symmetric under exchange of
j
and
k
. In the case
n=2
, Equation (
2.21
) reduces to:

F\cdot_{T}G:=F\cdot G+\sum_{k:=k_{1}+k_{2}\geq 1}\frac{\hbar^{k}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\langle F^{(k_{1},k_{2})},\Delta_{F,1}^{\otimes k_{1}}\Delta_{F,2}^{\otimes k_{2}}G^{(k_{1},k_{2})}\rangle,\quad F,G\in\text{Pol}_{loc}({\mathcal{M}}).

The information of the non-linear, interacting potential in Equation (
2.5
) is encoded at an algebraic level by means of two key objects: the
S
-matrix and the Bogoliubov map.

Remark 2.22
.

Recalling Equation (
2.5
), we will have to consider perturbative expansions with respect to three coupling parameters
\underline{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
with
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
and
\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}>9\lambda_{3}^{2}
. Unlike the single-scalar case, the interacting potential in Equation (
2.5
) cannot be treated as a power series in a single parameter. Instead, one must work with joint powers of the components of
\underline{\lambda}
. Consequently, factoring out the couplings from the potential is not convenient, and truncations at a given order are understood as truncations
jointly
in
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
.

In this context, we call
S-matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(V)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\left(\frac{i}{\hbar}\right)^{n}\underbrace{V\cdot_{T}\ldots\cdot_{T}V}_{n},

(2.22)

where all the couplings are incorporated in the interacting potential
V
. In addition,
\mathrm{Pol}_{\mu c}[[\lambda]](\mathcal{M})
is the space of functionals defined as a formal power series in the parameters
\underline{\lambda}:=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
, such that
F\in\mathrm{Pol}_{\mu c}[[\lambda]](\mathcal{M})
if
F=\sum\limits_{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{1}^{n_{1}}\lambda_{2}^{n_{2}}\lambda_{3}^{n_{3}}F_{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}
with
F_{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}\in\mathrm{Pol}_{\mu c}(\mathcal{M})
.

The S-matrix admits an inverse in the sense of formal power series – see also Remark
2.22
. For future convenience, it is useful to express it explicitly by means of the anti-Feynman parametrix:

\bm{H}_{AF}:=\bm{H}-i\bm{\Delta}_{R}=\bm{H}_{F}^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})),

(2.23)

where
\bm{H}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{M};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
is any bi-distribution which differs from the two-point correlation function of a Hadamard state by a smooth remainder, while
\ast
denotes the matrix and
L^{2}
formal adjoint. Thus, the inverse of
\mathcal{S}(\lambda V)
can be written in the form:

\mathcal{S}^{-1}(V):=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{n!}\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}\right)^{n}\underbrace{V\cdot_{\bar{T}}\ldots\cdot_{\bar{T}}V}_{n},

(2.24)

where
\cdot_{\bar{T}}
is the deformed product implemented by
\bm{H}_{AF}
. In addition the superscript
\star
indicates that
\mathcal{S}^{\star-1}
is the inverse of the S-matrix with respect to the product induced by
\bm{H}
, that is

\mathcal{S}(V)\star_{\bm{H}}\mathcal{S}^{-1}(V)=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(V)\star_{\bm{H}}\mathcal{S}(\lambda V)=\textbf{1},

where
1
is the identity functional as per Example
2.11
.

The S-matrix and its inverse are essential tools in constructing the Bogoliubov map, which serves to represent interacting observables – expressed as formal power series in the couplings – in terms of their free counterparts. In the case of two, mutually interacting scalar fields as per Equation (
2.4
), it can be defined in the following way. Given
F\in\mathrm{Pol}_{loc}(\mathcal{M})
, the Bogoliubov map is

R_{V}(F):=\mathcal{S}^{-1}(V)\star_{\bm{H}}[\mathcal{S}(\lambda V)\cdot_{T}F],\,\,\forall F\in\mathrm{Pol}_{loc}(\mathcal{M}).

(2.25)

where
\cdot_{T}
is the time ordered product as per Equation (
2.21
), whilst
\star_{\bm{H}}
is the deformed product implemented by the bi-distribution
\bm{H}
– see also Definition
2.19
.

We conclude the section with an example showing how to perform explicit computations by means of the Bogoliubov map.

Example 2.23
.

Consider the quadratic functionals as per Equations (
2.10
), (
2.11
) and (
2.9
) and let us discuss how to compute the expectation value of their interacting counterparts up to
\mathcal{O}(\underline{\lambda}^{3})
,
i.e.
, up to second order jointly in the coupling parameters. Barring replacing
\bm{H},\bm{H}_{F}
and
\bm{H}_{AF}
with the corresponding states, the expectation value of any observable corresponds to the evaluation at vanishing fields
\Phi:=(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=0
. We begin by expanding the S-matrix and its inverse in Equations (
2.22
) and (
2.24
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(V)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}+\frac{i}{\hbar}V-\frac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}V\star_{F}V+\mathcal{O}(\underline{\lambda}^{3}),

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{-1}(V)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}-\frac{i\lambda}{\hbar}V-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}V\star_{AF}V+\mathcal{O}(\underline{\lambda}^{3}).

(2.27)

Hence, suppressing the dependence on the field configurations for notational ease,

\displaystyle R_{V}(F[f])

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{O}(\underline{\lambda}^{3})}F[f]+\frac{i}{\hbar}\left(V[h]\cdot_{T}F[f]-V[h]\star_{H}F[f]\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[(V[h]\cdot_{\bar{T}}V[h])\star_{H}F[f]+(V[h]\cdot_{T}V[h])\cdot_{T}F[f]-2V[h]\star_{H}(V[h]\cdot_{T}F[f])\right],

(2.28)

where the functional
F\in\text{Pol}_{loc}({\mathcal{M}})
is taken to coincide either with
\Phi_{\ell}^{2}
,
\ell=1,2
or
(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})^{2}
. Notice that only maximally contracted terms contribute to the expectation value of
F
. To identify the relevant contributions modulus combinatorial factors, it is convenient to resort to a graphical approach. Henceforth, we shall denote in black the contractions between the fields
\varphi_{1}
, which are performed by means of
H_{1},H_{F,1}
and
H_{AF,1}
. We will use red to mark those between the fields of type
2
implemented by
H_{2},H_{F,2}
and
H_{AF,2}
. For simplicity, we shall discuss in detail only the case
F[f]=(\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2})^{2}[f]
, the other two following suit.
It is possible to infer that:

❈

none of the terms of the first order expansion survives. In fact, since the functional
F
is quadratic in the fields, in the term
V[h]\star_{\bm{H}}F[f]
as well as in
V[h]\cdot_{T}F[f]
, it is possible to perform at most two contractions. This leaves at least one power of
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
remaining,

❈

at second order in perturbation theory, from
V[h]\star_{\bm{H}}(V[h]\cdot_{T}F[f])
, one obtains three separate contributions, corresponding to the three different terms in the expression of
V[h]
. The first contributions can be pictorially represented as follows

\hbox to7.22pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.61111pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.61111pt}{-3.41666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{1}[h]\hbox to7.22pt{\vbox to6.83pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.61111pt\lower-3.41666pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.51276pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{{

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
{}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}
