\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})\vbox to28.00002pt{}{},

\propto\lambda_{3}^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,H_{1}(x,y)H_{2}(x,y)H_{1}(x,z)H_{2}(x,z)H_{F,1}(y,z)H_{F,2}(y,z)h(x)h(y)f(z).

We stress once again that all the contractions between elements within the round brackets are implemented by
\bm{H}_{F}
, while all others by
\bm{H}
.

❈

Focusing instead on
(V[h]\cdot_{T}V[h])\cdot_{T}F[f]
at a graphical level the same contractions contribute, hence we refrain from repeating the above analysis. The only difference with the previous case is that the non-vanishing contributions are related to different kernels and, thus, they correspond to the analytic expressions:

\displaystyle\propto\lambda_{1}\lambda_{3}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,

\displaystyle[H_{F,1}^{2}(x,y)H_{F,1}^{2}(x,z)H_{F,2}^{2}(y,z)

\displaystyle+H_{F,1}^{2}(x,y)H_{F,1}^{2}(y,z)H_{F,2}^{2}(x,z)]h(x)h(y)f(z),

\displaystyle\propto\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,

\displaystyle[H_{F,2}^{2}(x,y)H_{F,2}^{2}(x,z)H_{F,1}^{2}(y,z)

\displaystyle+H_{F,2}^{2}(x,y)H_{F,1}^{2}(x,z)H_{F,2}^{2}(y,z)]h(x)h(y)f(z).

The term
(V[h]\cdot_{\bar{T}}V[h])\star_{\bm{H}}F[f]
can be dealt with in a similar way by plugging in the above formulae the right parametrices.

Remark 2.24
.

Observe that the expressions in Example
2.23
is merely formal at the present stage, as they involve powers of the parametrices
\bm{H}_{F}
and
\bm{H}_{AF}
, which are ill-defined on the diagonal on account of Hörmander criterion
[
Hör90
]
. This requires a renormalisation procedure, which is inherently subject to ambiguities. Since they do not play a relevant rôle in the derivation of the results of this paper, we shall refrain from discussing them in detail.

Wetterich Equation in the Local Potential Approximation

In order to regularize the infrared behaviour of the theory, we introduce a scale-dependent mass regulator that suppresses low-energy modes:

Q_{k}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[q_{k,1}(x)T\chi_{1}^{2}(x)+q_{k,2}(x)T\chi_{2}^{2}(x)\right],

(2.29)

where, without loss of generality, we can fix
q_{k,1}(x)=q_{k,2}(x)=k^{2}f(x)
with
f(x)
a smooth cut off function, compactly supported in large regions of spacetime.

Remark 2.25
.

The choice of a regulator of this type may seem arbitrary to some extent, since nothing prevents us from adding also contributions proportional to the mixed product
\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}
. Yet, the choice in Equation (
2.29
) is dictated by the fact that the only coupling between the fields occurs at the level of the interaction potential via the term
\propto\lambda_{3}\varphi_{1}^{2}\varphi_{2}^{2}
. If a mass term
\propto\varphi_{1}\varphi_{2}
were also to be present, it would have been more convenient to retain in the expression of
Q_{k}
the mixed terms as well.

Denoting by

J(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}):=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\left[j_{1}(x)\chi_{1}(x)+j_{2}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\right],\,\,j_{1},j_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{M}),

an external current, we can introduce the (regularised) generating functional of the theory

Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})=\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+J+Q_{k})),

(2.30)

together with its connected counterpart

W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2}):=-i\log Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2}).

(2.31)

Here, we denote by
\omega
an arbitrary Hadamard state on the underlying quantum algebra, albeit not necessarily quasi-free for the free theory. Taking the first functional derivative of
W_{k}
, we obtain the
classical fields
, namely,

\frac{\delta W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})}{\delta j_{\ell}}=\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j_{\varphi_{1}},j_{\varphi_{2}})}\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star(S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}\chi_{\ell}(x)))=:\varphi_{\ell}(x),\quad\ell=1,2,

(2.32)

where
j_{\varphi_{\ell}},\ell=1,2
are the currents which solve the above equation as functions of
\varphi_{\ell}
. The
effective action
of the theory is then obtained as the Legendre transform with respect to both currents,
i.e.
,

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=W_{k}(j_{\varphi_{1}},j_{\varphi_{2}})-J_{\Phi}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}),

(2.33)

where
J_{\Phi}
is the functional defined in Equation (
2.1
) with
j_{\ell}\equiv j_{\varphi_{\ell}}
,
\ell=1,2
. By definition, the first functional derivative of
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}
yields the
quantum equations of motion
which are given by:

\frac{\delta\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}=-j_{\varphi_{\ell}},\quad\ell=1,2.

In a similar fashion as in
[
DP23
]
, one can also introduce the
average effective action

\Gamma_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})-Q_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}).

With all the necessary ingredients in place, we now turn to the derivation of the flow equations for the generating functionals, which take the form of differential equations in the scale parameter
k
. In particular, we derive the flow equation for the connected generating functional as well as that for the average effective action. The former is commonly known in the literature as
Polchinski equation

[
Pol84
]
and it reads

\partial_{k}W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})}\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star(S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T\chi^{2}_{\ell}(x))\right),

(2.34)

whilst the latter goes under the name of
Wetterich equation
and it takes the form

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Gamma_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})}\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)

\displaystyle\left[\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star(S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T\chi^{2}_{\ell}(x)\right)\right]-\varphi_{\ell}^{2}\}.

(2.35)

Observe that both the Polchinski and the Wetterich equations involve expectation values taken with respect to a chosen state
\omega
, which at this stage is not assumed to be quasi-free. However, since our ultimate interest lies in physically relevant states, we may, without loss of generality, restrict our attention to quasi-free ones. This assumption significantly simplifies the analysis, as it allows for the use of efficient approximation schemes to obtain approximate solutions to the flow equations. In particular, we adopt the following ansatz: we assume the existence of a local functional
U_{k}
, depending only on the fields and not on their derivatives, such that the average effective action can be written in the form

\Gamma_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left(\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\mu}\varphi_{1}(x)\nabla_{\mu}\varphi_{1}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\mu}\varphi_{2}(x)\nabla_{\mu}\varphi_{2}(x)+U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\right).

(2.36)

This approximation scheme is commonly referred to in the literature as
local potential approximation
(LPA), see
[
DDP+24
]
. Within such scheme, the left hand-side of Equation (
2.1
) becomes

\partial_{k}\Gamma_{k}=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}U_{k}.

For what concerns the right hand-side, the rationale is to truncate the effective action up to quadratic contributions in the fields. This can be achieved by considering an expansion of
\Gamma_{k}
around a classical solution
\varphi_{\ell,cl}
of the quantum equation of motion,
i.e.
,
\frac{\delta\Gamma_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}}|_{\varphi_{\ell,cl}}=0
, thus obtaining

\Gamma_{k}(\Phi)=\Gamma_{k}(\Phi_{cl})+\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{T}\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}(\Phi_{cl})\Phi+\mathcal{O}(|\Phi-\Phi_{cl}|^{3})=:\Gamma_{k}^{t}(\Phi)+\mathcal{O}(|\Phi-\Phi_{cl}|^{3}),

where
\Phi
is the field doublet as per Remark
2.5
, whilst
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}
is the Hessian matrix of
\Gamma_{k}
. Hence, we can define the
truncated action
corresponding to the effective action
\Gamma_{k}^{t}
as

I_{0}^{t}:=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left(\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\Phi)^{T}(\nabla\Phi)+\frac{1}{2}\Phi^{T}U_{k}^{(2)}(\Phi_{cl})\Phi\right).

In the above expression,
U_{k}^{(2)}(\Phi_{cl})
is the Hessian matrix of the truncated effective potential in Equation (
2.36
),
i.e.
,

U_{k}^{(2)}(\Phi_{cl})=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{1}^{2}}&0\\
0&\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{2}^{2}}\end{pmatrix},

(2.37)

where we have set the off-diagonal contributions to zero on account of the fact that no mixing occurs at the level of the masses. Thus, we can define the
effective mass of the theory

M:=I_{0}^{t}-I_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}d\mu_{x}\sum_{\ell=1,2}\left(\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}(\Phi_{cl})}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}^{2}}-m_{\ell}^{2}\right)\varphi_{\ell}^{2}(x)=:\sum_{\ell=1,2}M_{\ell}

where
I_{0}
is the free action of the theory as per Equation (
2.4
), while
M_{\ell}:=\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}^{2}}(\Phi_{cl})-m_{\ell}^{2}
for each
\ell=1,2
. This splitting of the effective mass suggests that at this level we can treat the two field theories as independent. As a consequence, applying the results in
[
DDP+24
]
for a single scalar field to this setting, we can write the Wetterich equation in the LPA as

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)(\Delta_{F,M_{\ell},k}(y,x)-H_{F,M_{\ell},k}(y,x)).

(2.38)

Here
\Delta_{F,M_{\ell},k}
is the Feynman propagator for the theory
I_{0}^{t}+Q_{k}
with
P_{k,\ell}:=\Box_{g}+\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}^{2}}
, while
H_{F,M_{\ell},k}
is the associated Feynman parametrix. Following the rationale detailed in
[
DDP+24
]
, observe that Equation (
2.38
) can be rewritten in terms of the difference between the symmetric part of the two-point function and the Hadamard parametrix of the theory described by the action
I_{0}+M+Q_{k}
,
i.e.
,

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)(\Delta_{S,M_{\ell},k}(y,x)-H_{M_{\ell},k}(y,x)).

(2.39)

Note that, up to this point, we have not make any assumption on the expression of the effective potential
U_{k}
, but we have simply require it to be a local functional of the fields. Yet, to compute explicitly the beta functions for the relevant couplings in Equation (
2.5
), such a choice needs to be specified. Heretofore, we will require that the potential
U_{k}
is of the form:

U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}):=U_{k,0}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})+\underbrace{\left(m_{1,k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{3,k}}{2}\rho_{2}+\frac{\lambda_{1,k}}{6}\rho_{1}\right)}_{M^{2}_{1,k}}\rho_{1}+\underbrace{\left(m_{2,k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{3,k}}{2}\rho_{1}+\frac{\lambda_{2,k}}{6}\rho_{2}\right)}_{M^{2}_{2,k}}\rho_{2},

(2.40)

where
\rho_{\ell}:=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\ell}^{2}
, for
\ell=1,2
.

Remark 2.26
.

The explicit expression of the effective potential
U_{k}
is dictated by the form of the interacting potential in Equation (
2.5
). Yet, notice that the splitting of the mutual interaction term
\frac{\lambda_{3,k}}{4}\varphi_{1}^{2}\varphi_{2}^{2}
is arbitrary, since nothing prevents us from incorporating it entirely in either one of the masses
M^{2}_{\ell,k}
. We stress that the choice made in Equation (
2.40
) reflects the intrinsic symmetry under exchange of the two fields, hence it is the most physically sensible.

In addition, the ensuing analysis will be tailored to the case of
4-
dimensional Minkowski spacetime so to have manageable, explicit expressions for the Hadamard parametrix of the underlying theory. If we choose as a reference state
\omega
the vacuum state, the right hand-side of Equation (
2.39
) takes the form,
cf.
,
[
BDF09
, Eq. (96)]
:

B:=\frac{k}{8\pi^{2}}(k^{2}+M_{k,1}^{2})\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+M_{k,1}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+\frac{k}{8\pi^{2}}(k^{2}+M_{k,2}^{2})\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+M_{k,2}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right),

where
\mu^{2}
is a reference energy scale, which encodes a residual renormalisation freedom. A direct computation yields the beta functions for the relevant couplings:

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}U_{k,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\sum_{\ell=1,2}(m^{2}_{\ell,k}+k^{2})\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{\ell,k}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)

(2.41)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}m_{k,\ell}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{\lambda_{\ell,k}}{6}\left[\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{\ell,k}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+1\right]+\frac{\lambda_{k,3}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{j,k}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right)+1\right]\right),\,\ell,j=1,2,j\neq\ell

(2.42)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}\lambda_{\ell,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{3k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{\lambda_{\ell,k}^{2}}{36}\frac{1}{k^{2}+m_{\ell,k}^{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{3,k}^{2}}{4}\frac{1}{k^{2}+m_{j,k}^{2}}\right],\quad\ell,j=1,2,j\neq\ell

(2.43)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}\lambda_{3,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\left[\frac{\lambda_{3,k}\lambda_{1,k}}{12(k^{2}+m_{1,k}^{2})}+\frac{\lambda_{3,k}\lambda_{2,k}}{12(k^{2}+m_{2,k}^{2}}\right].

(2.44)

The system of flow equations defines a set of coupled first-order differential equations which, for
k>0
and away from the singular loci
k^{2}+m^{2}_{\ell,k}=0
,
\ell=1,2
admits a unique local solution for given initial data on account of the Picard–Lindelöf theorem, as the beta functions are smooth in the couplings and continuous with respect to the scale parameter.

Remark 2.27
.

The local form of the right hand-side in Equation (
2.39
) and, in turn, the resulting beta functions in Equations (
2.41
)–(
2.44
) crucially depend on the dimension of the underlying spacetime. In generic dimension
d>2
, provided one can identify a suitable equilibrium quasi-free state, the above computations can be reproduced
mutatis mutandis
without any significant obstruction – see
[
DP23
, Sec. 6]
for additional examples in the case of a single scalar field.

We conclude this section by observing that, after a suitable rescaling, one may derive the corresponding flow equations for dimensionless couplings, see,
e.g.
,
[
DDP+24
, Sec. 6]
. In the same spirit as
[
DDP+24
, Sec. 6.2]
, one could also analyse the high-temperature limit
\beta\to 0
on Minkowski spacetime, as well as Bunch-Davies states on de Sitter spacetime. We shall not pursue these directions here, as they lie beyond the scope of the present work.

2.1.1
Stochastic fields via the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism

As a direct application of the formalism developed for two mutually interacting scalar fields, we shall discuss the case of a stochastic quantum field theory in the Martin-Siggia-Rose (MSR) formalism, see,
e.g.
,
[
BDD25
]
,
[
ZC02
]
,
[
Car20
]
. This approach allows to deal with fields whose dynamics is driven by a random potential, here specified by an additive Gaussian white noise. The idea underpinning the MSR formalism is to introduce an auxiliary field, which encompasses the stochastic correlations of the noise. Although originally designed to deal with stochastic differential equations (SDEs) or parabolic stochastic partial differential equations, this framework can be applied
mutatis mutandis
also to elliptic SPDEs. The key distinction lies in the interpretation of the auxiliary field: in the elliptic setting, it serves only as a Lagrange multiplier, rather than a genuine response field, as the absence of a temporal direction prevents the formulation of a causal structure.

Remark 2.28
.

In this work we do not address the question of the validity of the
Martin-Siggia-Rose representation for stochastic partial differential
equations. This issue involves analytic and probabilistic aspects which lie
beyond the scope of the present analysis, see
[
BDD25
]
. Instead, we shall take the MSR representation as a standing assumption and use it as the starting point for
the algebraic construction developed below.

On
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\delta)
, with
d\geq 1
, let
\xi
denote a white noise,
i.e.
, a Gaussian-centred random distribution taking values in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, whose correlations read

\mathbb{E}(\xi(f))=0,\,\,\,\mathbb{E}(\xi(f)\xi(g))=2D\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\quad\text{for all}\,f,g\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.45)

Here
D\in\mathbb{R}
denotes the
diffusion coefficient
and the factor
2
is purely conventional.
With a slight abuse of notation, we can equivalently rewrite Equation (
2.45
) at the level of distributional kernels, that is,

\mathbb{E}(\xi(x))=0,\,\,\,\mathbb{E}(\xi(x)\xi(y))=2D\delta(x-y).

(2.46)

In the aforementioned setting, consider the following non-linear elliptic stochastic PDE

(-\Delta+m^{2})\varphi+\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}=\xi,\quad m^{2}>0,\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(2.47)

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\equiv C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R})
be any smooth field configuration whose dynamics is governed by Equation (
2.47
). Via the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism applied to this case
[
MSR73
]
, we can replace the noise in Equation (
2.47
) with an auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which plays the rôle of a Lagrange multiplier enforcing Equation (
2.47
). The full action of the theory thus takes the form

I[\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\{\widetilde{\varphi}(x)\left[-\Delta\varphi(x)+m^{2}\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}(x)\right]-D\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}(x)\}.

(2.48)

On
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\delta)
, thanks to translational invariance of the underlying background and to Fourier techniques, the Helmholtz operator
L:=-\Delta+m^{2}
with
m^{2}>0
admits an exact inverse
G\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, whose integral kernel reads

G(x-y)\sim|x-y|^{2-d}e^{-m|x-y|},\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

modulus constant factors. More in general, on any smooth, connected and oriented Riemannian manifold
(\mathcal{N},h)
, the Helmholtz operator
L:=-\Delta+m^{2}
with
m^{2}>0
is elliptic. Therefore, locally in a neighbourhood
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{N}
, there exists a bi-distribution
G\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{O})
such that
L_{x}G(x,y)=L_{y}G(x,y)=\delta(x,y)
, where
\delta
is the Dirac delta distribution. Under the additional assumption that
(\mathcal{N},g_{\mathcal{N}})
is compact without boundary, the operator
L
is self-adjoint, elliptic and strictly positive for
m^{2}>0
on
L^{2}(\mathcal{N},\mu_{g_{\mathcal{N}}})
and, consequently, invertible. This implies that there exists a globally defined fundamental solution
G\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{N})
, which is exact and not modulus smoothing. In these settings, it has been proven in
[
DDR20
]
that the perturbative approach to both free and interacting quantum field theories extends to Euclidean scenario. In particular, it is possible to construct interacting observables via a counterpart of the Bogoliubov map, with the deformed product
\star
replaced by the pointwise product and the time-order one
\cdot_{T}
implemented in a similar way by the exact inverse
G
,
cf.
,
[
DDR20
, Sec. 7]
.
Yet, we refrain from pursuing this approach, as several technical and conceptual aspects of interacting Euclidean quantum field theories within the algebraic framework remain largely unexplored in the literature, such as the definition of time-ordered products in settings where only parametrices are available.
For these reasons, henceforth we shall focus on a counterpart of the action in Equation (
2.48
) living on
d-
dimensional Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\eta)
.

Remark 2.29
.

A priori
there is no obstruction to constructing such a field theory on a generic Lorentzian manifold
(\mathcal{M},g)
. However, the advantages of working on
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\eta)
are two-fold. On the one hand, on Minkowski spacetime we have a natural notion of vacuum state and, hence, an explicit, global expression for the Hadamard parametrix. As we shall see in the derivation of the Wetterich equation, this entails relevant simplifications in the computations. On the other hand, working with the flat metric, allows us to make explicit contact with the results obtained in
[
Duc25
]
, albeit via an alternative approach.

From this point onward, we shall denote by
P_{0}:=-\Box_{\eta}+m^{2}
the Green hyperbolic operator ruling the free dynamics of the scalar field
\varphi
. The corresponding action is given by

I[\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\{\widetilde{\varphi}(x)\left[-\Box_{\eta}\varphi(x)+m^{2}\varphi(x)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}(x)\right]-D\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}(x)\}.

(2.49)

In this Lorentzian construction, one of the fields
\widetilde{\varphi}
retains the rôle of an auxiliary, non-dynamical variable, and it is removed at the end of the computation by setting it equal to a constant.

Remark 2.30
.

In contrast with the case of the two, mutually interacting scalar fields investigated in Section
2.1
, the mass term in the action is of the form
m^{2}\varphi(x)\widetilde{\varphi}(x)
, hence it couples the dynamical and auxiliary fields. In spite of this fact, we can still introduce a field doublet of the form

\bm{\Phi}:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
\widetilde{\varphi}\end{pmatrix}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2}

and write the free action as a function of this doublet,
i.e.
,

I_{0}(\Phi)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\bm{\widetilde{\Phi}}^{T}(x)\mathrm{P}_{0}\bm{\Phi}(x),

where
\bm{\widetilde{\Phi}}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\bm{\Phi}
, while
\mathrm{P}_{0}:=P_{0}\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
.

Notice that, upon taking the functional derivative of the full action with respect to the field
\widetilde{\varphi}
, we obtain the equation of motion for
\varphi
,
i.e.
,

P_{0}\varphi+\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}-2D\widetilde{\varphi}=0.

(2.50)

The final term, which depends explicitly on the coefficient
D
, preserves a structure analogous to that induced by noise correlations. Nevertheless, in the Lorentzian formulation, it does not admit a genuine stochastic interpretation, as no underlying noise is present.
Observe, in addition, that one can associate to the auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}
a fictitious dynamics by considering the functional derivative of
I[\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}]
with respect to the dynamical field
\varphi
, namely,

P_{0}\widetilde{\varphi}+\lambda\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}=0.

(2.51)

This shows that the fictitious free dynamics of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is still ruled by the Klein-Gordon operator
P_{0}
with the
same
mass term for both fields.

Since several aspects of the ensuing discussion parallel those of Section
2.1
, we refrain from repeating the full analysis of the free and interacting theories, highlighting only the key differences. We denote by
\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})
the configuration space and by
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of functionals as per Definition
2.7
. This space can be equipped with a notion of Fréchet derivative as per Definition
2.9
, where now the first index refers to functional derivatives taken with respect to
\varphi
and the second one to those with respect to
\widetilde{\varphi}
. Being the interaction term polynomial in the fields, it suffices to confine our attention to polynomial functionals,
i.e.
, functionals with only a finite number of non-vanishing derivatives. In the same fashion as in Section
2.1
, we can also identify notable subspaces of
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{M})
, which will play a crucial rôle in the following. Since the definitions of
microcausal
,
regular
and
local functionals
remain exactly the same in this setting, we limit ourselves to give notable examples of elements lying in these classes.

Example 2.31
.

For future convenience, let us discuss some notable examples of polynomial functionals. For any pair of field configurations
(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for any test function
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define

\displaystyle\bm{1}[f](\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,f(x),

(2.52)

\displaystyle\Phi[f](\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\varphi(x)f(x),

(2.53)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}[f](\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\widetilde{\varphi}(x)f(x).

(2.54)

Observe that
\Phi^{(1,0)}[f](\varphi_{1};\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\varphi_{1}(x)f(x)
, all the higher functional derivatives being vanishing. Therefore, since
\text{supp}(\delta)\subset\text{Diag}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we conclude that
\Phi_{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. A similar argument entails that also
\widetilde{\Phi}_{f}\in\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Notice, in addition, that the functional defined by

(\Phi\widetilde{\Phi})[f](\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\varphi(x)\widetilde{\varphi}(x)f(x).

(2.55)

is local as well.

In a similar fashion as in Section
2.1
, we define the
classical microcausal algebra
of functionals as
\mathcal{A}_{cl}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=(\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\cdot,\ast)
, where
\cdot
denotes the pointwise product as per Equation (
2.13
). In order to encode into the algebraic structure the correlations of the underlying theory, we need to implement a deformation quantization procedure. More precisely, we want to allow only for contractions between the dynamical field configurations
\varphi
and the auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}
, so to account for stochastic fluctuations. To this avail, we introduce a deformation map of the form

D_{\hbar\Delta_{+}}:=\langle\hbar\Delta_{+},\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\widetilde{\varphi}}\rangle=\hbar\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}dxdy\,\Delta_{+}(x,y)\frac{\delta}{\delta\varphi(x)}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\widetilde{\varphi}(y)},

(2.56)

where
\Delta_{+}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Hadamard two-point function associated to the Klein-Gordon operator
P_{0}
. Then, for any pair of microcausal functionals
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, one can define their deformed product as in Equation (
2.17
), with the the deformation map chosen as per Equation (
2.56
). For the sake of doing computations, it is convenient to expand the exponential in a formal power series and write:

F\star G:=F\cdot G+\sum_{k,\widetilde{k}=1}^{\infty}\frac{\hbar^{(k+\widetilde{k})}}{k!\widetilde{k}!}\langle F^{(k,\widetilde{k})},(\Delta_{+})^{\otimes(k+\widetilde{k})}G^{(\widetilde{k},k)}\rangle.

(2.57)

Remark 2.32
.

Note that the deformed product in Equation (
2.57
) is symmetric under exchange of
F
and
G
,
i.e.
,
F\star G=G\star F
for any
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This is a by-product of the symmetry of Equation (
2.57
) with respect to
k,\widetilde{k}
and it naturally codifies the commutativity of the fields
\varphi
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
.

Example 2.33
.

Let us consider the local functionals introduced in Example
2.31
. We compute the deformed product between
\Phi_{f}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{f}
, as it will play a fundamental rôle in the interacting scenario: for any pair of field configurations
(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})\in\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and for any test function
f\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(\Phi[f]\star\widetilde{\Phi}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=(\Phi[f]\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})+\hbar\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}dxdy\,\Delta_{+}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y).

Since the deformation map is built in such a way to encode the commutativity of the underlying theory – see Remark
2.32
, it descends that

(\widetilde{\Phi}[f^{\prime}]\star\Phi[f])(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=(\Phi[f]\star\widetilde{\Phi}[f^{\prime}])(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}),

as one can directly check by applying Equation (
2.57
).

As for the case of two scalar fields, one can consider a bi-distribution
H\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which coincides with the Hadamard parametrix up to a smooth remainder, and define the local and covariant algebra of microcausal polynomial functionals with respect to the deformed product
\star_{H}
. The abstract Wick ordering of any local observable is then defined according to Equation (
2.19
), barring the replacement of
\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}(\bm{H})
with the Hadamard parametrix
H
of
P_{0}
.
Consider now an interaction potential of the form

V[h](\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\left(\frac{\lambda}{2}\varphi^{2}(x)\widetilde{\varphi}(x)+-D\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}(x)\right)h(x),\quad h\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(2.58)

Remark 2.34
.

Note that the potential in Equation (
2.58
) depends on two coupling parameters: the interaction parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and the diffusion coefficient
D\in\mathbb{R}
. Accordingly, all perturbative expansions are to be understood as joint expansions in both parameters.

For any pair of local, polynomial functionals
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we define their time-order product via the formal power series

F\cdot_{T}G:=F\cdot G+\sum_{k,\widetilde{k}=1}^{\infty}\frac{\hbar^{k+\widetilde{k}}}{k!\widetilde{k}!}\langle F^{(k,\widetilde{k})},\Delta_{F}^{\otimes(k+\widetilde{k})}G^{(\widetilde{k},k)}\rangle,

(2.59)

where we omit the field configurations for the sake of notational ease. In Equation (
2.59
),
\Delta_{F}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Feynman propagator of the free theory. Aside from the different form of the interacting potential, the
S-
matrix and the Bogoliubov map take a form analogous to that given in Equations (
2.22
) and (
2.25
). To further clarify the action of the Bogoliubov map in this setting, we provide an illustrative example.

Example 2.35
.

Let us compute the interacting counterpart of the functionals in Example
2.31
up to second order
jointly
in the couplings
\underline{\lambda}:=(\lambda,D)
as clarified in Remark
2.34
. Under the same
caveat
discussed in Example
2.23
, to recover the expectation value of the interacting observables it suffices to evaluate them at vanishing field configurations. Also in this case, only terms quadratic in the couplings may survive upon contraction, as the field content of the zeroth and first order contributions does not allow for maximally contracted diagrams. A direct inspection entails that due to the explicit structure of the interaction, functionals containing an equal number of dynamical and auxiliary fields have vanishing expectation. Therefore, we can confine our attention to the functionals
\Phi[f]
and
\widetilde{\Phi}[f]
, which are linear in the fields.

\displaystyle R_{V}(\Phi[f])|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi=0\\
\widetilde{\varphi}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=_{\mathcal{O}(\underline{\lambda}^{3})}\frac{1}{2\hbar^{2}}[2V[h]\star_{H}(V[h]\cdot_{T}\Phi[f])-(V[h]\cdot_{T}V[h])\cdot_{T}\Phi[f]

(2.60)

\displaystyle-(V[h]\cdot_{AT}\Phi[f])-(V[h]\cdot_{T}V[h])\star_{H}\Phi[f]],

(2.61)

and, analogously, for
\widetilde{\Phi}[f]

❈

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}[f]\vbox to19.00002pt{}{}\propto\lambda D\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3d}}dx\,dy\,dz\,H_{F}^{2}(x,y)H_{F}(y,z)h(x)h(y)f(z).

The fact that the interacting fields have non-zero expectations could have been deduced directly from Equation (
2.47
). Indeed, adding a stochastic current to the free equation of motion of a scalar field modifies the one-point function of the underlying theory. As a result, even selecting a Gaussian, quasi-free state,
\omega_{1}
no longer vanishes and it contributes to the two-point function.

Wetterich equation in the Local Potential Approximation

– In this paragraph, we shall derive the Renormalization Group flow equations in the local potential approximation for a quantum field theory described by Equation (
2.48
). The strategy is similar to the one detailed for the two scalar fields, albeit with some key differences we succinctly highlight in the following. The form of the deformation map - see Equation (
2.56
) – suggests a natural choice for the infrared regulator:

Q_{k}(\chi,\widetilde{\chi})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,q_{k}(x)\chi(x)\widetilde{\chi}(x),

(2.62)

where the factor
\frac{1}{2}
is added to account for the commutativity of the fields. Without loss of generality, we can fix
q_{k}(x):=k^{2}f(x)
, with
f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with support contained in large regions of spacetime. The regularised generating functional of the theory thus reads:

Z_{k}(j,\widetilde{j}):=\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+J+Q_{k})),

(2.63)

where

J(\chi,\widetilde{\chi}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,[\widetilde{j}(x)\chi(x)+j(x)\widetilde{\chi}(x)],\quad j,\widetilde{j}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is an external current, while
\omega
is a state on the quantum algebra, not necessarily quasi-free. The connected counterpart of the generating functional
Z_{k}
is given by

W_{k}(j,\widetilde{j}):=-i\log(Z_{k}(j,\widetilde{j}),

(2.64)

its first-order derivatives yielding the classical fields,
i.e.
,

\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta\widetilde{j}(x)}=:\varphi(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta j(x)}=:\widetilde{\varphi}(x).

(2.65)

The effective action and average effective action are

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}):=W_{k}(j,\widetilde{j})-J(j_{\widetilde{\varphi}},\widetilde{j}_{\varphi}),\quad\Gamma_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})-Q_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}),

(2.66)

with
j_{\widetilde{\varphi}}
and
\widetilde{j}_{\varphi}
the currents which solve Equation (
2.65
) as a function of the classical fields. In the present setting, the
Polchinski equation
is of the form:

\partial_{k}W_{k}(j,\widetilde{j})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j,\widetilde{j})}\partial_{k}q_{k}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star(S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T\eta\widetilde{\eta})(x)\right),

(2.67)

while the
Wetterich equation
reads:

\partial_{k}W_{k}(j,\widetilde{j})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j,\widetilde{j})}\partial_{k}q_{k}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star(S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T\eta\widetilde{\eta})(x)-\varphi(x)\widetilde{\varphi}(x)\right).

(2.68)

To find approximate local solutions to Equation (
2.68
), we work in the LPA scheme, detailed in Paragraph
2.1
. The key difference with respect to the analysis carried out for the two scalar fields is that the effective mass of the theory is now of the form

M:=I_{0}^{t}-I_{0}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}dx\,\left(\frac{\delta^{2}U_{k}}{\delta\varphi\delta\widetilde{\varphi}}(\varphi_{cl},\widetilde{\varphi}_{cl})-m^{2}\right)\varphi(x)\widetilde{\varphi}(x)

in which only the mixed second-order derivatives of the effective potential appear. As before, in order to obtain explicit expressions, we specify the analysis on
4-
dimensional Minkowski spacetime
(\mathbb{R}^{4},\eta)
and we select as
\omega
the vacuum state at zero temperature. Furthermore, we make the following ansatz on the structure of the effective potential
U_{k}
, namely, we assume that

U_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=U_{k,0}+m^{2}_{k}\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\varphi^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}-D_{k}\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}.

(2.69)

Following slavishly the steps detailed in Paragraph
2.1
, the Wetterich equation can be recast in the form of an ordinary differential equation for
U_{k}
with respect to the scale parameter
k
,
i.e.
,

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=\frac{k}{8\pi^{2}}(k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\varphi)\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\varphi}{\mu^{2}}\right).

(2.70)

Remark 2.36
.

The regularised mass term in Equation (
2.70
) is independent of both the auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}
and of the diffusion coefficient
D_{k}
. This feature is not accidental, but rather reflects the structural form of the MSR action – see Equation (
2.48
), where mass-like contributions arise solely in the mixed sector proportional to
\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}
. Within the local potential approximation, this implies that the second functional derivatives of the effective action and, hence, the regulator-modified inverse propagator, depend only on the field
\varphi
. Although alternative choices of regularisation could in principle be considered, the present one preserves the causal and response-field structure of the theory, ensuring that the auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}
retains its rôle as a response variable, while stochastic effects enter exclusively through correlation functions.

From Equation (
2.70
), one can directly deduce the beta functions for the relevant couplings:

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}U_{k,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}(k^{2}+m_{k}^{2})\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\right),

(2.71)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}m^{2}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\left[\log\left(\frac{k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}}{\mu^{2}}+1\right)\right],

(2.72)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}D_{k}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.73)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{2}}{4}\frac{1}{k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}}.

(2.74)

The auxiliary field
\widetilde{\varphi}
does not play the rôle of an independent running coupling in the flow equations above. Rather, the beta
functions in Equations (
2.71
), (
2.72
), (
2.73
) and (
2.74
) are obtained by projecting the Wetterich equation onto the corresponding monomials in the fields. In particular, the equations for
m_{k}^{2}
,
D_{k}
and
\lambda_{k}
are read off from the terms proportional to
\widetilde{\varphi}
. Hence, evaluating the auxiliary field on a constant
configuration, and in particular setting
\widetilde{\varphi}=1
, only removes an overall
k
-independent factor and does not modify the asymptotic behaviour of
the running couplings. The first equation, governing the field-independent part
U_{k,0}
of the effective potential, is by construction independent of

\widetilde{\varphi}
and is therefore unaffected by this projection.

Remark 2.37
.

The absence of a non-trivial flow for the noise amplitude
D_{k}
is a direct consequence of the Martin-Siggia-Rose structure of the theory. Indeed, the diffusion coefficient multiplies a purely quadratic term in the auxiliary field, whilst all interaction vertices remain linear in the physical field
\varphi
. As a result, within the present truncation, no loop diagrams can generate contributions proportional to
\widetilde{\varphi}^{2}
, owing also to the causal structure of the propagators. As a consequence, the RG flow does not induce any renormalisation of the noise sector, and
D_{k}
remains scale-independent. This property is stable under truncations preserving the MSR structure, but it may fail in the presence of multiplicative noise or higher-order couplings. This is consistent with the results obtained in
[
Duc25
]
, albeit via a different approach.

In order to directly compare our results with those derived in
[
Duc25
]
, we confine the analysis to the three-dimensional setting, namely, to
(\mathbb{R}^{3},\eta)
, and we require the reference state
\omega
to be the vacuum state at zero temperature. By
[
BDF09
, Eq. (95)]
, we have that the Hadamard function in the odd-dimensional setting can be written at the level of integral kernels as

H_{M_{k}}(x)=\frac{1}{4\sin(\frac{d}{2}-1)\pi}(2\pi)^{\frac{2-d}{2}}{M_{k}}^{\frac{d}{2}-1}|x^{2}|^{\frac{2-d}{4}}I_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(\sqrt{M_{k}^{2}|x^{2}|}),

for
x^{2}<0
and
d\geq 3
. Recalling that
I_{\nu}(y)\sim\frac{1}{\Gamma(\nu+1)}\left(\frac{y}{2}\right)^{\nu}
, with
F
an entire analytic function,
H_{M}
is smooth as a function of
M_{k}^{2}
.
In particular, setting
d=3
, we can write

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}H_{M_{k}}(x-y)=\frac{k}{4\pi}M_{k},\quad\text{with}\quad M_{k}=k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}\varphi,

where
U_{k}
is chosen as in Equation (
2.69
). Eliminating the auxiliary field by evaluating the final expressions at
\widetilde{\varphi}=1
, we thus obtain the beta functions for the relevant couplings:

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}U_{k,0}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{4\pi}(k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}),

(2.75)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}m_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{8\pi}\lambda_{k},

(2.76)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}D_{k}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{no scaling of the noise amplitude}

(2.77)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{no scaling of the coupling constant}

(2.78)

These results are in agreement with
[
Duc25
]
. The perturbative approach adopted in the present work, however, has the additional advantage that its validity extends beyond the subcritical regime,
i.e.
, for dimensions
d>3
, thereby enabling the study of the scaling behaviour of the relevant couplings on higher-dimensional backgrounds.

2.2
(B) Self-interacting Dirac fields

In this section, we discuss the algebraic quantum field theoretical framework applied to an interacting Fermionic model,
i.e.
, the Thirring model in dimension
d\geq 2
. The present approach differs from earlier work,
e.g.
,
[
Rej11
]
, in that the anticommutativity of Fermionic fields is implemented at the quantum level via the deformation map, rather than by introducing Grassmann variables. The present approach differs from earlier treatments, such as
[
Rej11
]
, in that no Grassmann-valued classical fields are introduced. Furthermore, we shall regard spinor and cospinor fields as independent field variables throughout the initial construction, imposing that they are related only at a later stage. This choice is particularly convenient for the perturbative algebraic formulation adopted below. The latter draws mainly on
[
BCD+24
]
, where an analogous framework is developed for the Thirring model, aside from the stochastic aspects considered therein, and on
[
DHP09
]
, where the algebraic quantization of the free Dirac field on arbitrary globally hyperbolic Lorentzian spacetimes is presented in detail.

Henceforth, we shall assume that the background
{\mathcal{M}}
is spin,
i.e.
, that its second Stiefel–Whitney class vanishes. This ensures the existence of at least one admissible spin structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
, see
e.g.

[
LM89
]
. To guarantee uniqueness, we further require that the spin bundle is trivial. Under these assumptions, the spinor and cospinor fields are smooth sections of the Dirac bundle and of its dual respectively, namely
\psi\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})
and
\bar{\psi}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
. Under the triviality assumptions, we have that
\psi,\bar{\psi}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\Sigma_{d}
, where
\Sigma_{d}=\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}
with
N_{d}:=2^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}
is the
spinor space
.

Notation 2.38
.

Also in this case we can introduce a convenient matrix notation – see Remark
2.5
. We define a field doublet
\Psi:=\begin{pmatrix}\psi\\
\bar{\psi}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
, where we use the fact that
\Sigma_{d}\simeq\Sigma_{d}^{*}
. Henceforth, we shall carefully distinguish between the
\mathbb{C}^{2}-
indices, denoted by Latin letters, and the Dirac indices, denoted by Greek letters. For instance,

\Psi_{i}=\begin{cases}\psi^{\rho}\quad\text{if}\,i=1\\
\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\quad\text{if}\,i=2\end{cases}\rho\in\{1,\ldots,N_{d}\}.

A key ingredient in the dynamical descriptions of Fermions are the gamma fields
\gamma\in\Gamma(T^{*}{\mathcal{M}}\otimes\text{End}(S{\mathcal{M}}))\simeq\Gamma^{\infty}(T^{*}{\mathcal{M}}\otimes\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}))
. Locally, fixing a global frame
\{e_{a}\}_{a=1,\ldots,N_{d}}
of
S{\mathcal{M}}
, they can be written in coordinate expression as
\gamma^{\mu}(x)=e_{a}^{\mu}(x)\gamma(e_{a})
, where
\gamma^{\mu}(x)\in\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}})
are the Dirac gamma matrices. In this context, the operators ruling the dynamics are the Dirac operator and its formal adjoint, which are explicitly given by

\displaystyle\not{D}^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=(i(\gamma^{\mu})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\nabla_{\mu}-m\mathbb{I}^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}):\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d})\rightarrow\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d}),

\displaystyle(\not{D}^{*})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}

\displaystyle:=(i(\gamma^{\mu})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\nabla_{\mu}+m\mathbb{I}^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}):\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma^{*}_{d})\rightarrow\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma^{*}_{d}).

These operators are Green hyperbolic and, hence, they admit unique advanced and retarded fundamental solutions
\Delta^{\psi}_{A/R},\Delta^{\bar{\psi}}_{A/R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d}\otimes\Sigma_{d}^{*})
, which can be built out of the ones for the Klein-Gordon operator via the Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula – see
[
LM89
]
. Concretely, if
\Delta_{A/R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}})
denote the advanced and retarded propagators for a real, scalar field on
({\mathcal{M}},g_{{\mathcal{M}}})
,
\Delta^{\psi}_{A/R}=\not{D}^{*}\Delta_{A/R}
and
\Delta^{\bar{\psi}}_{A/R}=\not{D}\Delta_{A/R}
are those associated to the Dirac operator and to its formal adjoint, respectively. The Hadamard two-point functions for the Dirac field can thus be obtained from a scalar Hadamard two-point function
\Delta_{+}
by applying the Dirac operator and inserting the appropriate spinorial parallel transport. We shall denote these by

(\Delta^{\psi}_{+})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}},(\Delta^{\bar{\psi}}_{+})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d}\otimes\Sigma_{d}^{*})\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}}),

or we can equivalently write, in a more compact matrix notation

\bm{\Delta}_{+}^{\Psi}:=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta^{\psi}_{+}&0\\
0&-\Delta^{\bar{\psi}}_{+}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}},\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}})),

where the minus sign is included for later convenience.

The full action of the Thirring theory is thus specified by
I_{0}[\psi,\bar{\psi}]=I_{0}[\psi]+I_{0}[\bar{\psi}]
, with

\displaystyle I_{0}[\psi]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[i\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x)(\gamma^{\mu})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x)\nabla_{\mu}\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}(x)-m\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x)\psi^{\rho}(x)-\frac{\lambda}{2}(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)(x)(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi)(x)\right]f(x),

(2.79)

\displaystyle I_{0}[\bar{\psi}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[i\psi^{\rho}(x)(\gamma^{\mu})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(x)\nabla_{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x)-m\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x)\psi^{\rho}(x)-\frac{\lambda}{2}(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)(x)(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi)(x)\right]f(x).

(2.80)

Here
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
is the coupling constant, while
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
is a smooth, compactly supported cut off.

For the sake of notational brevity, let us denote by
V:=\Sigma_{d}
and let us introduce the vector space
W:=\bigoplus_{k,k^{\prime}\geq 0}(V^{\otimes k}\otimes(V^{*})^{\otimes k^{\prime}})
with
V^{\otimes 0}=(V^{*})^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{C}
by convention. In the present setting, the configuration space is the Fréchet space
\mathcal{E}_{V}({\mathcal{M}}):=\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};V)\oplus C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};V),
endowed with the product topology. Furthermore, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})
the space of functionals
u:\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow W
, which are smooth in the Bastiani sense – see.

Remark 2.39
.

To be precise, such
u
should be understood as a functional-valued distribution,
i.e.
,
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathcal{M};\mathcal{F}_{W}(\mathcal{M}))
. With a slight abuse of notation, we may still write
u\in\mathcal{F}_{W}(\mathcal{M})
, when referring to the evaluated functional
u(f;\cdot,\cdot)
for a given but fixed test function
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
.

Remark 2.40
.

Bearing the above remark in mind, let
\{e_{\rho}\}_{\rho=1,\ldots,N_{d}}
be an arbitrary basis of
V
with dual counterpart given by
\{e^{\rho}\}_{\rho=1,\ldots,N_{d}}
. Fixed
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
, for any pair of field configurations
(\psi,\bar{\psi})\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\times\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
, we can define the component of
u
with values in
W^{(p,q)}=V^{\otimes p}\otimes(V^{*})^{\otimes q}
as

u^{\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{p}}_{\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{q}}(f;\psi,\bar{\psi}):=\langle u(f;\psi,\bar{\psi}),e^{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e^{\lambda_{p}}\otimes e_{\rho_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{\rho_{q}}\rangle_{V},

(2.81)

where we indicate by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{V}
the duality pairing between
V
and
V^{*}
. It descends that, exploiting the natural embedding
W^{(p,q})\hookrightarrow W
for
p,q\geq 0
, any
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}};\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}}))
can be decomposed as

u=\sum_{p,q\geq 0}u^{\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{p}}_{\rho_{1}\ldots\rho_{q}}e_{\lambda_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e_{\lambda_{p}}\otimes e^{\rho_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes e^{\rho_{q}},

(2.82)

where the right hand-side is as per Equation (
2.81
).

The space
\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})
can be equipped with a notion of Fréchet derivative: for any
u\in\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})
, we define its
(k,k^{\prime})-
functional derivative as

\displaystyle u^{(k,k^{\prime})}:\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\otimes\ldots\otimes\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\otimes\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\otimes\ldots\otimes\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\times\mathcal{E}_{V}({\mathcal{M}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow W

\displaystyle u^{(k,k^{\prime})}(\eta_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\eta_{k}\otimes\bar{\eta}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\bar{\eta}_{k^{\prime}};\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{k+k^{\prime}}}{\partial s_{1}\ldots\partial s_{k}\partial\bar{s}_{1}\ldots\partial\bar{s}_{k}}u(s_{1}\eta_{1}+\ldots+s_{k}\eta_{k}+\psi;\bar{s}_{1}\bar{\eta}_{1}+\ldots+\bar{s}_{k^{\prime}}\bar{\eta}_{k^{\prime}}+\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle\big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1}=\ldots=s_{k}=0\\
\bar{s}_{1}=\ldots=\bar{s}_{k^{\prime}}=0\end{subarray}}.

(2.83)

In particular, if
\exists(\widetilde{k},\widetilde{k}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
u^{(k,k^{\prime})}=0
for any
k\geq\widetilde{k}
or
k^{\prime}\geq\widetilde{k}^{\prime}
, we say that
u
is a polynomial functional of the fields,
i.e.
,
u\in\text{Pol}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})
.

Remark 2.41
.

Recall that for any
u\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}},\text{Pol}_{W}({\mathcal{M}}))
–
cf.
, Remark
2.39
–,
u(\psi,\bar{\psi})\equiv u(\cdot,\psi,\bar{\psi})
is a vector-valued distribution for any pair of field configurations
(\psi,\bar{\psi})\in\mathcal{E}_{V}({\mathcal{M}})
. We thus consider the following non-polarised definition of the wavefront set of
u
:

\text{WF}(u(\psi,\bar{\psi})):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\text{dim}W}WF(u_{i}(\psi,\bar{\psi})),\quad u_{i}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}},\mathcal{F}({\mathcal{M}})),

where
u_{i}
denotes the
i-
th component of
u
with respect to a chosen basis of
V
.

In light of the previous remark, we can introduce the following notable subspaces of
\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})
:

❈

We call
microcausal functionals
the elements of

\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{u\in\mathcal{F}_{W}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,\text{WF}(u^{(k,k^{\prime})}(\psi,\bar{\psi}))\subseteq G_{k+k^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}),\forall k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\},

(2.84)

where

G_{k+k^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}})=T^{*}(\underbrace{{\mathcal{M}}\times\ldots\times{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+k^{\prime}-\text{times}}\setminus\left(\bigcup_{x\in{\mathcal{M}}}(V^{+}_{x})^{k+k^{\prime}}\cup\bigcup_{x\in{\mathcal{M}}}(V^{-}_{x})^{k+k^{\prime}}\right)),

V^{\pm}_{x}
denoting the future (
resp
. past) pointing light cones at
x\in{\mathcal{M}}
.

❈

We call
regular functionals
the elements of

\mathcal{F}_{reg,W}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{u\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,u^{(k,k^{\prime})}(\psi,\bar{\psi})\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}^{k+k^{\prime}}),\forall k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

(2.85)

❈

Denoting by
\text{supp}(u(\psi,\bar{\psi})):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\text{dim}W}\text{supp}(u_{i}(\psi,\bar{\psi}))
, we call
local functionals
the elements of the space

\mathcal{F}_{loc,W}({\mathcal{M}}):=\{u\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}})\,|\,\text{supp}(u^{(k,k^{\prime})}(\psi,\bar{\psi}))\subseteq\text{Diag}_{k+k^{\prime}}({\mathcal{M}}),\forall k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

(2.86)

The
classical microcausal algebra
of functionals is the unital, commutative,
*-
algebra
\mathcal{A}^{W}_{cl}({\mathcal{M}}):=(\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}}),\cdot,\ast)
of microcausal functionals as per Equation (
2.84
), endowed with the pointwise product as per Equation (
2.13
) and with an involution given by the complex conjugation.

Example 2.42
.

Central to the construction of the quantum theory are functionals that are linear in the fields,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\psi^{\rho}(x)f(x),\quad\Psi:=\Psi^{\rho}e_{\rho}\in\text{Pol}_{V}({\mathcal{M}}),

(2.87)

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x)f(x),\quad\bar{\Psi}:=\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}e^{\rho}\in\text{Pol}_{V^{*}}({\mathcal{M}}),

(2.88)

where
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
,
(\psi,\bar{\psi})\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\times\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
, whilst
\{e_{\rho}\}
and
\{e^{\rho}\}
are as per Remark
2.40
. It follows by direct inspection that these functionals are both local and microcausal and, hence, they form the building blocks of the classical algebra
\mathcal{A}^{W}_{cl}
. Henceforth, we shall implicitly identify
\Psi
(
resp
.
\bar{\Psi}
) with its component
\Psi^{\rho}
(
resp
.
\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}
) and write
\Psi^{\rho}\in\text{Pol}_{V}({\mathcal{M}})
(
resp
.
\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}\in\text{Pol}_{V^{*}}({\mathcal{M}})
).

Observe that, at the present stage, the classical algebra is commutative per construction. To incorporate the information on the CAR into the algebraic structure, a convenient strategy consists in selecting a deformation map which is sensitive to the ordering of fields within functionals. Consider

\displaystyle D_{\hbar\Delta^{\psi}_{+}}

\displaystyle:=\langle\hbar\Delta^{\psi}_{+},\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{\psi}}\rangle=\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,(\Delta_{+}^{\psi})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi^{\rho}(x)}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}(y)},

\displaystyle D_{\hbar\Delta^{{\bar{\psi}}}_{+}}

\displaystyle:=\langle\hbar\Delta^{\bar{\psi}}_{+},\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{\psi}}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}\rangle=\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,(\Delta_{+}^{\bar{\psi}})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}\frac{\delta}{\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x)}\otimes\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}(y)},

and define the deformation map as

D_{\hbar\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\bm{\Delta}^{\Psi}_{+}}:=D_{\hbar\Delta_{+}^{\psi}}-D_{\hbar\Delta_{+}^{\bar{\psi}}}.

(2.89)

Notice that the minus sign in Equation (
2.89
) naturally encodes the anticommutativity of the spinor fields, thus avoiding the need to introduce Grassmann variables. The deformed product is thus given by Equation (
2.17
), with
\bm{\Delta}_{+}
replaced by
\bm{\Delta}^{\Psi}_{+}
as in Equation (
2.89
). Formally, for any pair of microcausal functionals
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}})
, we can write

F\star G=F\cdot G+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1\\
k=k_{1}+k_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{k_{2}}\hbar^{k}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\langle F^{(k_{1},k_{2})},(\Delta_{+}^{\psi})^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes(\Delta_{+}^{\bar{\psi}})^{\otimes k_{2}}G^{(k_{2},k_{1})}\rangle,

(2.90)

where the Fréchet derivatives are as per Equation (
2.83
).

Example 2.43
.

As an example, we show that Equation (
2.90
) yields the expected correlations between spinor fields. More precisely, a direct computation entails

\displaystyle\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})\star\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})+\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,(\Delta_{+}^{\psi})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y),

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})\star\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})-\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,(\Delta_{+}^{\bar{\psi}})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y),

where
\Psi^{\rho}[f]
and
\bar{\Psi}_{p^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}]
are the linear functionals in Example
2.42
, with
f,f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
.
Notice that, by construction, the ordering of the fields becomes relevant: whenever a cospinor field appears as the first argument of the deformation map, a minus sign arises in the correlations.

In this setting, consider globally-defined bi-distributions
H^{\psi},H^{\bar{\psi}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}},\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}}))
such that they locally coincide up to smoothing with the Hadamard two-point functions
\Delta_{+}^{\psi}
and
-\Delta_{+}^{\bar{\psi}}
, respectively. Then, the local and covariant algebra of microcausal polynomial functionals is the anti-commutative, quantum algebra obtained by deformation with respect to
H^{\psi}
and
H^{\bar{\psi}}
. The Wick-ordering of Fermionic observables is then implemented similarly to the scalar case – see Section
2.1
and, in particular, Equation (
2.19
).

Interacting theory
. Let us consider a self-interacting Dirac field theory on
({\mathcal{M}},g_{{\mathcal{M}}})
with an interaction of the form:

V[h](\psi,\bar{\psi}):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{\lambda}{2}(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi)h(x),\quad h\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}}),\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}.

(2.91)

For any pair of microcausal functionals
F,G\in\mathcal{F}_{\mu c,W}({\mathcal{M}})
, we define their time-ordered product as

F\cdot_{T}G:=F\cdot G+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1},k_{2}\geq 1\\
k=k_{1}+k_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{k_{2}}\hbar^{k}}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\langle F^{(k_{1},k_{2})},\Delta_{F,\psi}^{\otimes k_{1}}\otimes\Delta_{F,\bar{\psi}}^{\otimes k_{2}}G^{(k_{2},k_{1})}\rangle,

(2.92)

where for notational ease we avoid to indicate the field configurations. Here,
\Delta_{F,\psi},\Delta_{F,\bar{\psi}}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}))
denote the Feynman propagators for the Dirac operator and its adjoint, respectively. Note in addition the minus sign in Equation (
2.92
), which arises from the ordering of the spinorial fields within the functionals.

Example 2.44
.

For the sake of clarity, we illustrate an explicit application of Equation (
2.92
). Consider the linear functionals
\Psi^{\rho}\in\text{Pol}_{V}({\mathcal{M}})
and
\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}\in\text{Pol}_{V^{*}}({\mathcal{M}})
and let us compute

\displaystyle\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot_{T}\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi)}+\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta_{F,\psi}(x,y)f(x)f^{\prime}(y),

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot_{T}\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}[f^{\prime}](\psi,\bar{\psi})\cdot\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})-\hbar\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\Delta_{F,\bar{\psi}}(x,y)f^{\prime}(x)f(y).

To define the interacting counterpart of a given vector-valued functional
F\in\text{Pol}_{loc,W}({\mathcal{M}})
, we can exploit the action of the Bogoliubov map in Equation (
2.25
), with the potential
V
as per Equation (
2.91
). Interacting observables are expressed as formal power series in the coupling parameter
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, and truncating the series at a given order yields an approximation of the interacting observable to that perturbative order as illustrated by the ensuing example.

Example 2.45
.

In analogy with the analysis of two mutually interacting scalar fields,
cf
., Example
2.23
, we now compute the expectation value of the interacting counterparts of the following functionals up to
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})
:

\displaystyle\Psi^{\rho}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi}),\quad(\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}\Psi^{\rho})[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\psi^{\rho}(x)\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x)f(x),

\displaystyle\Psi^{2}[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})\equiv(\Psi^{\rho}\Psi^{\rho^{\prime}})[f](\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\psi^{\rho}(x)\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}(x)f(x),

for any
f\in\mathcal{D}({\mathcal{M}})
and
(\psi,\bar{\psi})\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\times\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
. By direct inspection,
R_{V}(\Psi^{\rho}[f])\big|_{\psi,\bar{\psi}=0}=_{\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})}0
. Due to the fact that the interacting potential contains an even number of fields, we deduce that
R_{V}(\Psi^{\rho}[f])\big|_{\psi,\bar{\psi}=0}=_{\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{\infty})}0
at any perturbative order. In a similar fashion, one can show that
R_{V}(\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}[f])\big|_{\psi,\bar{\psi}=0}=_{\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{\infty})}0
and, more in general,

R_{V}((\bar{\Psi}^{n}\Psi^{m})[f])\big|_{\psi,\bar{\psi}=0}=_{\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{\infty})}0

whenever
n\neq m
. Therefore, the only potentially non-vanishing expectation values should arise from functionals with symmetric spinorial content. Let
F[f]:=(\bar{\Psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}\Psi^{\rho})[f]
and

\displaystyle R_{V}(F)\big|_{\psi,\bar{\psi}=0}=_{\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\hbar^{2}}\left[\underbrace{2V[h]\star_{\bm{H}}(V[h]\cdot_{T}F)}_{(\text{a})}-\underbrace{(V[h]\cdot_{T}V[h])\cdot_{T}F[f]}_{(\text{b})}-\underbrace{(V[h]\cdot_{AT}V[h])\star_{\bm{H}}F[f]}_{(\text{c})}\right].

Let us compute separately each term.

(a)

Adopting the same graphical notation as in Example
2.23
, the first contribution corresponds to

\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}[f])\vbox to25.00002pt{}{},

where denote in red those contractions performed via
H^{\psi}
or
H_{F}^{\psi}
, whilst we indicate in black those contractions implemented by
-H^{\bar{\psi}}
or
-H_{F}^{\bar{\psi}}
, depending on the contribution under scrutiny. In the above, the Dirac indices are omitted for the sake of notational clarity. At the level of integral kernels this yields, up to combinatorial factors,

\propto\lambda^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,(H_{F}^{\psi})^{2}(x,y)H^{\bar{\psi}}(x,y)H^{\bar{\psi}}(x,z)H_{F}^{\psi}(y,z)h(x)h(y)f(z),

(2.93)

where, with a slight abuse of notation, we have suppressed spinor indices and omitted the explicit appearance of Dirac gamma matrices. More precisely,
(H_{F}^{\psi})^{2}:=(H_{F}^{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}H_{F}^{\psi}\gamma_{\mu})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}
and the corresponding integral kernel should be understood as involving the trace over Dirac indices of such terms.

(b)
