which, at the level of integral kernels, read

\propto\lambda^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,(H_{F}^{\bar{\psi}})^{2}(x,y)H_{F}^{\psi}(x,y)H_{F}^{\psi}(x,z)H_{F}^{\bar{\psi}}(y,z)h(x)h(y)f(z),

and

\propto\lambda^{2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{3}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}d\mu_{z}\,(H_{F}^{\psi})^{2}(x,y)H_{F}^{\bar{\psi}}(x,y)H_{F}^{\bar{\psi}}(x,z)H_{F}^{\psi}(y,z)h(x)h(y)f(z),

respectively.

(c)

The analysis of the last term is identical to
(\text{b})
barring minor modifications, and, hence, we omit the discussion.

Wetterich equation in the Local Potential Approximation

In this section, we derive the Polchinski and Wetterich equations for self-interacting Dirac fields. The strategy closely parallels that used for two mutually interacting scalar fields; therefore, we limit ourselves to highlighting only the essential steps.

We begin by introducing an infrared regulator of the form:

Q_{k}(\eta,\bar{\eta}):=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[T(\eta^{\rho}[q_{k,\psi}]_{\rho}{}^{\rho^{\prime}}\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}})(x)-T(\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}}[q_{k,\bar{\psi}}]^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}\eta^{\rho})(x)\right],

(2.94)

for any
(\eta,\bar{\eta})\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}})\times\Gamma^{\infty}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
, where
T(\cdot)
denotes the time-ordering of the expression within brackets. Without loss of generality, we can choose
[q_{k,\psi}]_{\rho}^{\rho^{\prime}}=[q_{k,\bar{\psi}}]^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}=k^{2}\mathbb{I}_{\rho}^{\rho^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, we consider the external current

J(\eta,\bar{\eta}):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\bar{j}_{\rho}(x)\eta^{\rho}(x)+\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}j^{\rho}(x)\bar{\eta}_{\rho}(x),\quad(j,\bar{j})\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(D{\mathcal{M}})\times\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}}).

(2.95)

In full analogy to the scalar theory, the regularise generating functional
Z_{k}
and its connected counterpart
W_{k}
are thus given by

Z_{k}(j,\bar{j}):=\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+J+Q_{k})),\quad W_{k}(j,\bar{j}):=-i\log Z_{k}(j,\bar{j}),

(2.96)

with
\omega
a state on the quantum algebra, not necessarily quasi-free. The first-order functional derivatives of
W_{k}
yield the classical fields,
i.e.
,

\begin{cases}\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta\bar{j}_{\rho}(x)}=:\psi^{\rho}(x),\\
\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta j^{\rho}(x)}=:\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(x),\end{cases}

while
(j_{\psi})^{\rho},(j_{\bar{\psi}})_{\rho}
are the currents which solve the above system as a function of
\bar{\psi}_{\rho},\psi^{\rho}
, respectively. The connected, regularised generating functional is the key ingredient in the
Polchinski equation
, which reads

\displaystyle\partial_{k}W_{k}(j,\bar{j})

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j,\bar{j})}\left[(\partial_{k}q_{k,\psi})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star[S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T(\eta^{\rho}\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}})]\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j,\bar{j})}\left[(\partial_{k}q_{k,\bar{\psi}})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star[S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T(\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}}\eta^{\rho})]\right)\right].

(2.97)

Upon taking the Legendre transform of
W_{k}
in both variables, one obtains the regularised effective action

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=W_{k}(j_{\psi},j_{\bar{\psi}})-J_{\Psi}(\psi,\bar{\psi}),

where
J_{\Psi}
is the functional in Equation (
2.95
) with the currents replaced by
j_{\psi}
and
j_{\bar{\psi}}
. From
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}
, one can derive the quantum equations of motion:

\begin{cases}\frac{\delta\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}}{\delta\psi^{\rho}}=-(j_{\bar{\psi}})_{\rho},\\
\frac{\delta\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}}{\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho}}=-(j_{\psi})^{\rho}.\end{cases}

Crucial for the derivation of the Wetterich equation is the regularised average effective action specified by

\Gamma_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi}):=\widetilde{\Gamma}_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})-Q_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi}).

The
Wetterich equation
thus reads

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Gamma_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\frac{1}{Z_{k}(j,\bar{j})}

\displaystyle\left[(\partial_{k}q_{k,\psi})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star[S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T(\eta^{\rho}\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}})]-T(\psi^{\rho}\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}})\right)\right.

\displaystyle+\left.(\partial_{k}q_{k,\bar{\psi}})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(x)\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star[S(V+J+Q_{k})\cdot_{T}T(\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}}\eta^{\rho})]-T(\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}\psi^{\rho})\right)\right].

(2.98)

In the local potential approximation, we can express the average effective action as

\Gamma_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\not{D}^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}+\psi^{\rho}(\not{D}^{*})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}+U_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})\right],

(2.99)

where
U_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})
is a suitable polynomial in the fields. Expanding it around classical field configurations, we obtain

\Gamma_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=\Gamma_{k}(\psi_{cl},\bar{\psi}_{cl})+\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{T}\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}(\psi_{cl},\bar{\psi}_{cl})\bar{\Psi}+\mathcal{O}(|\Psi-\Psi_{cl}|^{3}),

where
\Psi\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
is the spinor doublet as per Notation
2.38
, while
\psi_{cl},\bar{\psi}_{cl}
are such that
\frac{\delta\Gamma_{k}}{\delta\psi^{\rho}}\big|_{\psi=\psi_{cl}}=0
and
\frac{\delta\Gamma_{k}}{\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho}}\big|_{\psi=\psi_{cl}}=0
. Here, we denote by
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}(\psi,\bar{\psi})
the Hessian matrix of
\Gamma_{k}
, taking values in the vector space
W
. As discussed at the beginning of Section
3.2
, with a suitable choice of basis,
\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}
can be written in such a way that the mixed derivatives lie on the diagonal. In this setting, the effective mass of the theory is given by

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=I_{0}^{t}[\psi,\bar{\psi}]-I_{0}[\psi,\bar{\psi}]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}[U_{k,\psi\bar{\psi}}^{(2)}(\psi,\bar{\psi})-m_{k}\mathbb{I}]^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}}_{(M_{\psi})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}}\psi^{\rho}(x)\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x)\right)+\left(\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}[U_{k,\bar{\psi}\psi}^{(2)}(\psi,\bar{\psi})+m_{k}\mathbb{I}]^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}}_{(M_{\bar{\psi}})^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}}\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x)\psi^{\rho}(x)\right)\right].

(2.100)

We can choose the effective potential to be of the form

U_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=U_{0,k}+m_{k}\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}-m_{k}\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}\bar{\psi}_{\rho}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)\bar{\psi}_{\rho}(\gamma_{\mu})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}.

(2.101)

Notice that, being the relevant couplings the same for both the spinor and cospinor fields, we can discard the second term in the previous equation to derive the flow equations.
Henceforth, we shall specialise the analysis to the case of
2-
dimensional Minkowski spacetime,
i.e.
,
(\mathbb{R}^{2},\eta)
. In this setting,

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathbb{I}^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}\left[\left(\Delta_{F,M_{\psi},k}^{\psi}(y,x)-H_{F,M_{\psi},k}^{\psi}(y,x)\right)^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}-\left(\Delta_{F,M_{\bar{\psi}},k}^{\bar{\psi}}(y,x)-H_{F,M_{\bar{\psi}},k}^{\bar{\psi}}(y,x)\right)^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\right],

and, focusing specifically on the first contribution, we have

\partial_{k}U_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathbb{I}^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}\left(\Delta_{F,M_{\psi},k}^{\psi}(y,x)-H_{F,M_{\psi},k}^{\psi}(y,x)\right)^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}.

(2.102)

In the two-dimensional setting, for the vacuum state, this reduces to

\partial_{k}U_{k,\psi\bar{\psi}}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathbb{I}^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(\not{D}^{*})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}}\left(\Delta_{S,M,k}^{\infty}(y,x)-H_{M,k}^{\psi}(y,x)\right)=\frac{k^{2}}{2}\mathbb{I}^{\rho^{\prime}}_{\rho}(M_{k})^{\rho}_{\rho^{\prime}},

where
M_{k}
is a suitable effective mass, here defined by
M_{k}:=k^{2}+m_{k}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)\gamma_{\mu}
. We thus obtain the following set of beta functions for the relevant couplings:

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}U_{0,k}

\displaystyle=kM_{k}\bigg|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=0\\
\bar{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}=k(k^{2}+m_{k})

(2.103)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}m_{k}

\displaystyle=k^{2}\frac{\delta^{2}M_{k}}{\delta\psi^{\rho}\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho}}\bigg|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=0\\
\bar{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}=k^{3}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{2}

(2.104)

\displaystyle k\partial_{k}\lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=2k^{2}\frac{\delta^{4}M_{k}}{\delta\psi^{\rho}\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\delta\psi^{\rho^{\prime}}\delta\bar{\psi}_{\rho^{\prime}}}\bigg|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=0\\
\bar{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}=0.

(2.105)

Remark 2.46
.

The vanishing of the beta function for the self-interaction coupling in the three-dimensional Dirac model is compatible with simple dimensional considerations. In
d=3
, the Dirac field has canonical dimension
[\psi]=\frac{(d-1)}{2}=1
, so that the quartic Thirring interaction,
i.e.
,

(\bar{\psi}\gamma^{\mu}\psi)(\bar{\psi}\gamma_{\mu}\psi)

has dimension
4
. Its coupling therefore has negative mass dimension,
[\lambda]=-1
and, therefore, it is not marginal. Within the local truncation used here, no logarithmic contribution is generated which could induce a non-trivial quantum running of this coupling. The corresponding beta function therefore vanishes. If one instead introduces a dimensionless coupling, its scale dependence will be purely canonical at this level of approximation.

3
Existence of local solutions to the Renormalization Group flow equations

The issue we shall address in this section concerns whether it is possible to establish local existence of solutions to the Renormalization Group (RG) flow equations. To tackle this problem, it is convenient - albeit not mandatory - to work on an ultra-static spacetime
\mathcal{M}
,
\text{dim}({\mathcal{M}})=d
, so to have explicit and manageable expressions for the advanced and retarded propagators of the free theory. Furthermore, we shall assume that the background
{\mathcal{M}}
is spin, namely, that its second Stiefel–Whitney class vanishes. This ensures the existence of at least one admissible spin structure on
{\mathcal{M}}
. To guarantee uniqueness, we further require that the spin bundle
S{\mathcal{M}}
is trivial.

As before, let
E\equiv E[{\mathcal{M}},\pi,\mathbb{K}^{n}]
be a trivial
\mathbb{K}
-vector bundle, where
\mathbb{K}
denotes either
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, over
{\mathcal{M}}
,
i.e.
,
E
is globally diffeomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{K}^{n}
. Smooth sections of this bundle
\Gamma^{\infty}(E)\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{K}^{n}
correspond to field multiplets, where
\otimes
is the algebraic tensor product and
n\in\mathbb{N}
depends on the model under scrutiny. For the sake of readability, we separate the analysis of the two cases under consideration:
(A)
two mutually interacting scalar fields, see Section
3.1
and
(B)
self-interacting Dirac fields, see Section
3.2
. Furthermore, we shall succinctly comment on how to extend this formalism to stochastic fields in the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism,
cf.
, Section
3.1.1
.

3.1
(A) Two mutually interacting scalar fields

Consider two, mutually interacting scalar fields on
{\mathcal{M}}
whose dynamics is governed by Equation (
2.4
). In this scenario, the dynamical fields
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}}):=C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{R})
can be equivalently regarded as a single smooth section of the trivial (real) vector bundle
E:={\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus, we can introduce the field doublet

\Phi:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{1}\\
\varphi_{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})\simeq\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}})\simeq\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
\mathcal{E}_{2}({\mathcal{M}}):=\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})\oplus\mathcal{E}({\mathcal{M}})
. The Latin indices will be employed to indicate the components of such doublet.

Notation 3.1
.

In the following, we shall denote by
\mathcal{E}:=L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
and let
A\in\text{Mat}(2;\mathcal{E})
be such that each matrix entry
A_{ij}
is a trace-class operator on
L^{2}({\mathcal{M}})
for all
i,j=1,2
. Assume in addition that the action of such an operator on
\Psi\in\mathcal{E}
is given by

(A\Psi)_{i}(x):=\sum_{j=1,2}(A_{ij}\Psi_{j})(x)=\sum_{j=1,2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{y}\,A_{ij}(x,y)\Psi_{j}(y),\,\forall i=1,2.

We define the
Hilbert space trace of
A
on
\mathcal{E}
as

\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}(A):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}A(x,x).

(3.1)

We stress that if
A
in not trace-class, Equation (
3.1
) is valid only at a formal level.

In the same spirit of Section
2.1
, we shall consider an infrared mass regulator of the form

Q_{k}(\chi_{1},\chi_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[q_{k,1}(x)T\chi_{1}^{2}(x)+q_{k,2}(x)T\chi_{2}^{2}(x)\right],

and an external current

J(\chi_{1},\chi_{2}):=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\left[j_{1}(x)\chi_{1}(x)+j_{2}(x)\chi_{2}(x)\right],\,\,j_{1},j_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{M}).

Additionally, let us denote by

\mathrm{P}_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}P_{0,1}&0\\
0&P_{0,2}\end{pmatrix}\,\,\text{and}\,\,\mathsf{q}_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}q_{k,1}&0\\
0&q_{k,2}\end{pmatrix},

(3.2)

where
P_{0,\ell}:=-\Box_{g}+m_{\ell}^{2}+\xi_{\ell}R
,
\ell=1,2
. Furthermore, let

Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})=\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+Q_{k}+J)),\quad W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})=-i\log Z_{k}(j_{1},j_{2}),

(3.3)

be the generating functional of the underlying regularized theory and its connected counterpart, respectively. For future convenience, we introduce the following short-hand notation: for any interacting operator
F
, we shall denote its weighted expectation value by

\langle F\rangle:=e^{-iW_{k}}\omega\left(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+Q_{k}+J)\cdot_{T}F\right),

(3.4)

where the deformed and time-ordered products are defined as per Equations (
2.17
) and (
2.21
), whilst
V
denotes the interacting potential.
A direct computation entails that the Hessian of
W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})
takes the form

[W_{k}^{(2)}(j_{1},j_{2})](x,y)=i\begin{pmatrix}\langle\chi_{1}(x)\cdot_{T}\chi_{1}(y)\rangle-\varphi_{1}(x)\varphi_{1}(y)&\langle\chi_{1}(x)\chi_{2}(y)\rangle-\varphi_{1}(x)\varphi_{2}(y)\\
\langle\chi_{2}(x)\chi_{1}(y)\rangle-\varphi_{2}(x)\varphi_{1}(y)&\langle\chi_{2}(x)\cdot_{T}\chi_{2}(y)\rangle-\varphi_{2}(x)\varphi_{2}(y)\end{pmatrix},

(3.5)

where we recall that
\varphi_{\ell}
are the classical fields,
i.e.
,
\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta j_{\ell}(x)}=:\varphi_{\ell}(x)
,
{\ell}=1,2
. This implies that we can recast the
Polchinski equation
in the form:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}W_{k}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\left[\partial_{k}\mathsf{q}_{k}:(W_{k}^{(2)}+\Phi\otimes\Phi):\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\left[\partial_{k}\mathsf{q}_{k}:(W_{k}^{(2)}+\Phi\otimes\Phi):\right](x,x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\left[-iW_{k,\ell}^{(2)}(x,y)+\varphi_{l}(x)\varphi_{l}(y)-\widetilde{H}_{F,\ell}(x,y)\right],

where
-iW_{k,\ell}^{(2)}(x,y)
is the propagator of the interacting theory for the field
\varphi_{\ell}
, whilst
\widetilde{H}_{F,\ell}(x,y)
,
{\ell}=1,2
, are the counter-terms stemming from the point-splitting regularization. In the above formula,
\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}
is as per Equation (
3.1
) – see also Notation
3.1
.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that, by construction, the off-diagonal contributions in Equation (
3.5
) do not appear at the level of the Polchinski equation. Yet, they still affect the diagonal contributions when taking the matrix inverse.

Thus, denoting by
G_{k,\ell}(x,y):=-iW_{k,\ell}^{(2)}(x,y)
, the
Renormalization Group (RG) flow equation
reads:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x):G_{k,\ell}(x,x):_{\widetilde{H}_{F,\ell}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\left(G_{k,\ell}(x,y)-\widetilde{H}_{F,\ell}(x,y)\right),

(3.6)

where in the last line the normal-ordering prescription has been made explicit. We can now define the
effective potential

U_{k}
by means of the relation:

\Gamma^{(2)}_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})-\mathsf{q}_{k}:=\mathrm{P}_{0}+U^{(2)}_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}),

(3.7)

where
U_{k}^{(2)}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
is the associated Hessian matrix. The operator appearing on the right hand-side of Equation (
3.7
) is referred to in the literature as
quantum wave operator
.
Setting
[(\cdot)^{(2)}]_{\ell j}:=\frac{\delta^{2}(\cdot)}{\delta\varphi_{\ell}\varphi_{j}}
, Equation (
3.7
) can be equivalently written componentwise as

\begin{cases}[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell\ell}-q_{k,\ell}=:P_{0,\ell}+[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell\ell},\\
[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}=:[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}\,\,\,\,\text{for}\,\,\ell\neq j.\end{cases}

(3.8)

Let

\Delta_{A/R}=\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{A/R,1}&0\\
0&\Delta_{A/R,2}\end{pmatrix},

\Delta_{A/R,\ell}
being the advanced/retarded Green operators associated to
P_{0,\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
. In a similar fashion as in
[
DP23
]
, we shall work in the
local potential approximation
, namely, we assume that
U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
is a multiplicative (scalar) operator, depending on polynomials of the fields but not on their derivatives, that is,

\displaystyle U_{k}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,u(\varphi_{1}(x),\varphi_{2}(x),k)f(x),\,\,f\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle[U_{k}^{(2)}(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})]_{\ell j}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\partial^{2}_{\ell j}u(\varphi_{1}(x),\varphi_{2}(x),k)f(x)\delta(x,y),

(3.9)

where
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{M}
is a compact region of spacetime such that
\text{supp}(f)\subseteq\mathcal{O}
. Under this assumption, the operator
\mathrm{P}_{k}:=\mathrm{P}_{0}+U^{(2)}_{k}
is still Green hyperbolic, as off-diagonal contributions – being of order zero – do not affect the principal symbol. Therefore,
\mathrm{P}_{k}
admits unique advanced and retarded Green operators

\Delta^{U}_{A/R}:\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})\rightarrow\Gamma^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})

such that

\mathrm{P}_{k}\circ\Delta^{U}_{A/R}=\text{id}|_{\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})},\,\,\,\,\Delta^{U}_{A/R}\circ\mathrm{P}_{k}|_{\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})}=\text{id}|_{\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})},

and, for every
\psi\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

\text{supp}\,\Delta^{U}_{A/R}(\psi)\subset J^{\mp}(\text{supp}\psi).

Observe that, on account of the Schwartz kernel theorem, we can associate to the above Green operators matrix-valued bi-distributions
\Delta^{U}_{A/R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
,
i.e.
,

\Delta^{U}_{A/R}=\begin{pmatrix}[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{11}&[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{12}\\
[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{21}&[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{22},\end{pmatrix}

each entry lying in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}})
.

Notation 3.3
.

With a slight abuse of notation, we denote by
\Delta^{U}_{A/R}
both the Green operators and the corresponding distributional kernel, since we feel that no confusion can arise.

Remark 3.4
.

Although
[U^{(2)}_{k}]_{12}=[U^{(2)}_{k}]_{21}
due to the commutativity of the functional derivatives, it is
not
true in general that
[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{12}=[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{21}
. The reason is that the advanced and retarded propagators are not symmetric in their entries. Hence, we can only conclude that, at level of integral kernels,
[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{12}(x,x)=[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{21}(x,x)
.

We can now introduce the advanced and retarded Møller operators associated to
\mathrm{P}_{k}:=\mathrm{P}_{0}+U_{k}^{(2)}
. These intertwine the free and quantum wave operators and they are specified by

(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{A})\,\,\text{and}\,\,(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)}).

(3.10)

Using the Møller operators, we can recast Equation (
3.1
) in the form of a differential equation for the effective potential:

\partial_{k}U_{k}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)[(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})w(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{A})]_{\ell\ell}(x,x),

(3.11)

where,

w:=\begin{pmatrix}w_{11}&0\\
0&w_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})),

with
w_{\ell\ell}(x,y):=\Delta_{F,\ell}(x,y)-H_{F,\ell}(x,y),
for each
\ell=1,2
.

Henceforth, we shall assume that the classical fields
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
are constant. This implies that the integral kernel of the effective potential
u(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},k)
together with its second derivatives in Equation (
3.1
) are constant functions with respect to the variable
x
. In contrast with the strategy detailed in the previous section, we make the following two choices:

❈

In order to further simplify the analysis, we shall assume that the infrared regulator
Q_{k,\ell}
has an integral kernel with a linear dependence on
k
, that is:

q_{k,\ell}(x):=(k_{0,\ell}+\epsilon k)f(x),\,\,\ell=1,2

where
f
is the adiabatic cutoff function in Equation (
3.1
) and
\epsilon>0
is chosen to be the same for both fields. Without loss of generality, we shall set
\epsilon\equiv 1
and assume that
k\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Observe, in addition, that
the constant contribution
\propto k_{0,\ell}
can be conveniently reabsorbed in the free mass of the theory and, hence, it can be discarded.

❈

We shall refrain ourselves from expanding the effective average action around a fixed background solution. Specifically, we do not need to truncate the resulting expression up to quadratic contributions in the fields, but we can keep the full non-linear structure in the effective potential.

Employing the short-hand notation
u^{(2)}:=(\partial^{2}_{\ell j}u)_{\ell j}
, Equation (
3.11
) can thus be recast as an initial, boundary value problem for
u(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},k)
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{k}u=\sum_{\ell=1,2}G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)})=:\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{G}_{k}(u^{(2)}))\\
u(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},a)=\psi\\
u|_{\partial X\times[a,b]}=\beta\end{cases}

(3.12)

where
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a compact set containing all possible values of
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
,
k\in[a,b]\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, whilst
\psi\in C^{\infty}(X)
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\partial X\times[a,b])
are assigned functions. In the above equation, the functional
G_{k,\ell}
is defined for each
\ell=1,2
as

G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)}):=-\frac{1}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\left[(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R}f)\otimes(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R}f)(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}(x,x),

(3.13)

where
\Delta^{u}_{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
is the retarded fundamental solution of the Green hyperbolic operator
\mathrm{P}_{0}+fu^{(2)}
.

Notation 3.5
.

Consider matrix-valued bi-distributions
A_{1},A_{2},W\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))\simeq\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}})\times\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})
. Since in Equation (
3.13
) we are interested in the coinciding point limit, we can decompose

W(x,y):=\sum_{i,j=1,2}W_{ij}(x,y)E_{ij},

where
i,j
are matrix indices,
W_{ij}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}})
, while
(E_{ij})_{ab}:=\delta_{ia}\delta_{jb}
is the Weyl basis. Thus, we can write at the level of integral kernels

A_{1}(x,x^{\prime})\otimes A_{2}(y,y^{\prime})=\sum_{i_{1},j_{1}=1,2}\sum_{i_{2},j_{2}=1,2}(A_{1})_{i_{1}j_{1}}(x,x^{\prime})(A_{2})_{i_{2}j_{2}}(y,y^{\prime})E_{i_{2}j_{2}}E_{i_{1}j_{1}}

and

\left[A_{1}(x,x^{\prime})\otimes A_{2}(y,y^{\prime})\right](W)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}=1,2}(A_{1})_{i_{1}i}(x^{\prime},x)(A_{2})_{i_{2}j}(y^{\prime},y)W_{ij}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})E_{i_{1}i_{2}},

where we used the property
(E_{i_{2}j_{2}})^{T}=E_{j_{2}i_{2}}
and
E_{i_{1}j_{1}}E_{ij}=\delta_{j_{1}i}E_{i_{1}j}
. Henceforth, we shall compactly write

\displaystyle[(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})(W)]_{\ell\ell}(x,y):=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{2}}d\mu_{x}^{\prime}d\mu_{y}^{\prime}\,(A_{1})_{\ell i}(x,x^{\prime})(A_{2})_{\ell j}(y,y^{\prime})W_{ij}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}).

Remark 3.6
.

Observe that Equation (
3.13
) can be equivalently written as follows:

G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)}):=-\frac{1}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x):G_{k,\ell}:(x,x),

(3.14)

where

:G_{k,\ell}:(x,y):=[(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{u}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})w(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{A})]_{\ell\ell}(x,y),\,\,\ell=1,2.

To establish local existence of solutions to Equation (
3.12
), the strategy consists in applying the
Nash-Moser Theorem
in the Hamiltonian formulation,
cf.

[
Mos66a
,
Mos66b
,
Nas56
]

[
Ham82
, Part III]
. We recall it here for the reader’s convenience – see also Appendix
A
for further details.

Theorem 3.7

(Nash-Moser Theorem in Hamilton formulation)
.

Consider two tame Fréchet spaces
F
and
G
and let
R:\mathcal{U}\subset F\rightarrow G
be a tame map. Under the additional requirements:

1.

The map
DR:\mathcal{U}\times F\rightarrow G
, i.e., the first functional derivative of
R
, admits a unique inverse
E:\mathcal{U}\times G\rightarrow F
such that if
DR(u)v=f
, then
E(u)f=v
for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
and
f\in G
,

2.

the inverse
E
is a smooth tame map,

R
is locally invertible and
R^{-1}
is tame smooth.

In order to exploit Theorem
3.7
, we shall identify in our setting the tame map playing the role of
R
and prove it abides by the conditions
1.
and
2.
in the above statement. For the sake of clarity, we shall divide the following analysis in separate steps.

Step 0: Preliminary definitions
. Let
\Phi:=(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})\in X
and define
F:=C^{\infty}(X\times[a,b])
which is a Fréchet space with respect to the family of seminorms:

||u||_{n}=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}\sup_{\Phi,k}|D^{\alpha}u(\Phi,k)|,

(3.15)

where the derivatives
D^{\alpha}
are taken both with respect to the fields and to the parameter
k
. The space
F
is also tame, being the space of smooth functions over a compact set. Consider the tame Fréchet subspace

F_{0}:=\{u\in F\,|\,u(\Phi,a)=0,u|_{\partial X\times[a,b]}=0\}\subset F

and decompose the solution
\widetilde{u}
of Equation (
3.12
) as

\widetilde{u}=u_{b}+u,\,\,u\in F_{0},

where
u_{b}\in F
is such that it abides by the initial and boundary conditions,
i.e.
,
u_{b}(\Phi,a)=\psi
and
u_{b}|_{\partial X\times[a,b]}=\beta
. Assume in addition that there exists a positive constant
A>0
such that
||u||_{4}\leq A
. This requirement will prove crucial in deriving the sought norm estimates.

Step 1: The Renormalization Group operator is tame smooth
.
In this context, the map in Theorem
3.7
is given by the Renormalization Group (RG) operator,
i.e.
,

\mathcal{RG}:F_{0}\subset F\rightarrow F,\,\,\,\mathcal{RG}(u):=\partial_{k}(u+u_{b})-\sum_{\ell=1,2}G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)}+u_{b}^{(2)}),

(3.16)

where
G_{k,\ell}
is as per Equation (
3.13
). In order to show that
\mathcal{RG}
is tame smooth in a suitably small neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}
of
0
, it suffices to prove that
G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)})
,
\ell=1,2
, is a tame smooth map for
\widetilde{u}\in u_{b}+\mathcal{U}
. To this avail, we need to devise the counterparts of
[
DP23
, Lem. 4.1]
to this setting.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a ultra-static spacetime. Given
g\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})
, consider

h=\Delta^{U}_{R}g.

Then,
h
is a past compact smooth section with compact support on any Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma
. In addition, since

h=(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})\varphi,

where
\varphi=\Delta_{R}g
, the following estimates hold true:

i.

there exist positive constants
c,C^{\prime}>0
such that
C^{\prime}
depends on the support of
f
in
U_{k}
but not on
\widetilde{u}
, while
c
does not depend on
U_{k}
and

||h||^{t}_{L^{\infty}}\leq c||h||^{t}_{H^{2}}\lesssim c\,||\varphi||^{t}_{H^{2}}\exp({C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{\infty}})\lesssim c||\varphi||^{t}_{H^{2}}\exp({C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2}}),

(3.17)

where
||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{\infty}:=\max_{\ell=1,2}\sum_{j=1,2}|\widetilde{u}^{(2)}|_{\ell j}
is the induced
\ell^{\infty}
operator norm on
\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})
and the symbol
\lesssim
corresponds to
\leq
modulus irrelevant numerical factors, which can be reabsorbed in a redefinition of the constants
c,C^{\prime}
.

ii.

there exist a positive constant
\widetilde{C}>0
depending only on the support of
g
such that

||h||^{t}_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim c\exp({C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2}})\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,(t-\tau)||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}\lesssim\widetilde{C}\,\exp({C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2}})\sup_{\tau\leq t}||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}},

(3.18)

where
c,C^{\prime}>0
are as per item
i.

In the above estimates, we denote by
||\cdot||^{t}_{L^{\alpha}}
the norm on
L^{\alpha}(\Sigma_{t};\mathbb{C}^{2})
, given for
\alpha\in[1,\infty)
by

||h||^{t}_{L^{\alpha}}:=\left(\int_{\Sigma_{t}}d\mu_{\textnormal{{x}}}\,|h(t,\textnormal{{x}})|^{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha}},

where
d\mu_{\textnormal{{x}}}
is the measure induced by the Riemannian metric on the Cauchy hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
at a fixed time
t
, whilst for
\alpha=\infty
, we set

||h||^{t}_{L^{\infty}}:=\text{ess}\sup_{\textnormal{{x}}\in\Sigma_{t}}|h(t,\textnormal{{x}})|,

|h(t,\textbf{x})|
being the
\mathbb{C}^{2}-
norm of
h
. Similarly,
||\cdot||^{t}_{H^{2}}
is the norm on
H^{2}(\Sigma_{t};\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

Proof.

(
i.
) A direct consequence of
\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{P}_{k}
being Green hyperbolic is that
\Delta_{R}
and
\Delta^{U}_{R}
both map past compact smooth sections to past compact smooth sections. This entails that
h,\varphi\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{pc}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Recalling also that

\Delta^{U}_{R}=\Delta_{R}(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{R})=(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})\Delta_{R}.

Note that, since
U_{k}^{(2)}=f\widetilde{u}^{(2)}
, where
f
is a smooth, compactly supported function and
\widetilde{u}^{(2)}
is constant on
{\mathcal{M}}
, we have the following:

h=\Delta_{R}g-\Delta_{R}U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{R}g=\Delta_{R}g-\Delta_{R}U_{k}^{(2)}h=\varphi-\Delta_{R}U_{k}^{(2)}h,

or, equivalently, componentwise we can write

h_{\ell}=\varphi_{\ell}-\sum_{j=1,2}[\Delta_{R}U_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}h_{j}=\varphi_{\ell}-\sum_{j=1,2}\Delta_{R,\ell}[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}h_{j},\,\,\ell=1,2.

Denote by
D_{\Sigma_{t}}
the Laplace operator on
\Sigma_{t}
and define
\omega_{\ell}:=\sqrt{D_{\Sigma_{t}}+m^{2}_{\ell}}
for
\ell=1,2
. Thus,

h_{\ell}(t,\textbf{x})=\varphi_{\ell}(t,\textbf{x})-\sum_{j=1,2}[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,\frac{\sin(\omega_{\ell}(t-\tau))}{\omega_{\ell}}(fh_{j})(\tau,\textbf{x}).

As in
[
DP23
, Lem. 4.1]
, we can conclude that, for each
\ell=1,2
,

\displaystyle||h_{\ell}||^{t}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq||\varphi_{\ell}||^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum_{j=1,2}|[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}|\int_{a}^{t}d\tau\,(t-\tau)||f||^{\tau}_{L^{\infty}}||h_{j}||^{\tau}_{L^{2}},

\displaystyle||h_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}+C\sum_{j=1,2}|[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}|\int_{a}^{t}d\tau||h_{j}||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}

where the norms here are on
L^{\alpha}(\Sigma_{t})
and
H^{2}(\Sigma_{t})
, respectively,
C>0
is a positive constant independent on
\widetilde{u}^{(2)}
, while
a\in\mathbb{R}
depends on
f
. Applying the Grönwall lemma in integrated form,
cf.
,
[
Grö19
]
, we obtain

||h_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}\,\exp({C\sum_{j=1,2}|[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}|}),\,\,\,\ell=1,2.

Now, recalling that,

||h||^{t}_{L^{2}}=\left(\sum_{\ell=1,2}(||h_{\ell}||^{t}_{L^{2}})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\sum_{\ell=1,2}||h_{\ell}||^{t}_{L^{2}}\leq\sum_{\ell=1,2}\left[||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}+C\sum_{j=1,2}|[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}|\int_{a}^{t}d\tau||h_{j}||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}\right],

an, passing to the Sobolev space norm, we get

\displaystyle||h||^{t}_{H^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}\,\exp(C\sum_{j=1,2}|[\widetilde{u}^{(2)}]_{\ell j}|)\leq\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}\,\exp(C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}||\varphi||^{t}_{H^{2}}\exp(C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{\infty}).

In a similar fashion as in
[
DP23
, Lem. 4.1]
, one can show that there exists
c>0
independent of
U_{k}
such that

||h||^{t}_{L^{\infty}}\leq c||h||^{t}_{H^{2}}.

The estimate in Equation (
3.17
) thus follows.

(
ii.
) To prove the estimate in Equation (
3.18
), it suffices to note that

\varphi_{\ell}(t,\textbf{x})=[\Delta_{R}g]_{\ell}(t,\textbf{x})=\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,\frac{\sin(\omega_{\ell}(t-\tau))}{\omega_{\ell}}g_{\ell}(\tau,\textbf{x}),

where we exploit the fact that the matrix-valued bi-distribution
\Delta_{R}
is diagonal. Hence, for each
\ell=1,2
, it holds that

||\varphi_{\ell}||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,(t-\tau)||g_{\ell}||^{\tau}_{H^{2}},

which entails

||\varphi||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,(t-\tau)\sum_{\ell=1,2}||g_{\ell}||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}\leq\sqrt{2}\int_{-\infty}^{t}d\tau\,(t-\tau)||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}},

where in the last line we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

Observe that, in order to prove that the RG operator as per Equation (
3.16
) is tame smooth, it is sufficient to show that the maps
G_{k,\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
in Equation (
3.13
) are tame smooth. This is indeed the case, as granted by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.9
.

The functionals
G_{k,\ell}
,
\ell=1,2
are smooth functions of
\widetilde{u}^{(2)}
and, in particular, they are tame smooth for
\widetilde{u}\in u_{b}+\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

For the sake of clarity, we divide the proof in two parts.

Part 1:
G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)})
is smooth
. A direct consequence of the explicit choice of
q_{k,\ell}
, is that
G_{k,\ell}
depends on
(\Phi,k)
only through
\widetilde{u}^{(2)}
,
i.e.
,
G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)})(\Phi,k)=G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k))
.
The
n-
th order functional derivative of
\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}):=G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)})
with respect to
\widetilde{u}
thus reads

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}^{(n)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u};v_{1},...,v_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{\bm{m}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left((\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{m_{i}}f\otimes(\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{1-m_{i}}f\right)(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}(x,x)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n+1}}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\sum_{\bm{m}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\left[(\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})\otimes(\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})\circ\right.

\displaystyle\left.\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left((v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{m_{i}}f\otimes(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{1-m_{i}}f\right)(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}(x,x),

where the sum is over the
2^{n}
choices of
\bm{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
with
m_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, while we define
A^{0}=id
for any linear operator
A
such that
A(x)\in\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C})
,
x\in{\mathcal{M}}
. Due to the compactness of the supports of
f
and
q_{k,\ell}
and thanks to the smoothness of
w
, one has that the integral in the above expression yields a finite result. Thus, we can conclude that
G_{k,\ell}
is smooth as a function of
\widetilde{u}\in F
.

Part 2.
G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)})
is tame smooth
.
To show that
G_{k,\ell}
is tame, we need to estimate

\displaystyle||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{n}:=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq n}\sup_{\Phi,k}|D^{\alpha}G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k))|=||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}+\underbrace{\sum_{1\leq|\alpha|\leq n}\sup_{\Phi,k}|D^{\alpha}G_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k))|}_{(1)},

in terms of the seminorm
||\widetilde{u}||_{n+r}
for a certain
r\in\mathbb{N}
. Term
(1)
can be more conveniently rewritten using the functional version of the
Faà di Bruno formula
, that is

\displaystyle|D^{\alpha}\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}(\Phi,k))|

\displaystyle=|\sum_{1\leq p\leq|\alpha|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{p}=\alpha\\
|\alpha_{i}|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}(\Phi,k))(D^{\alpha_{i}}\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k),\ldots,D^{\alpha_{p}}\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k))|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha}\sum_{p\leq|\alpha|}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{p}=\alpha\\
|\alpha_{i}|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\sup_{\Phi,k}|D^{\alpha_{i}}\widetilde{u}^{(2)}(\Phi,k)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha}\sum_{p\leq|\alpha|}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{p}=\alpha\\
|\alpha_{i}|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{|\alpha_{i}|}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha}\sum_{p\leq|\alpha|}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||^{p}_{|\alpha|-p+1},

where we used the estimate
1\leq|\alpha_{i}|=|\alpha|-\sum_{j\neq i}|\alpha_{j}|\leq|\alpha|-p+1
. Observe that in the above equation we have
formally
defined

||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}:=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1},\ldots,v_{p}\in F\\
||v_{i}||_{2}=1\end{subarray}}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u};v_{1},\ldots,v_{p})||_{0},\,\,\,\text{for any $p\in\mathbb{N}$},

for
\widetilde{u}\in u_{b}+\mathcal{U}
with
\mathcal{U}
a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
0
.
Hence, calling
m:=|\alpha|
, we have

||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{n}<||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{p=1}^{m}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||^{p}_{m-p+1},

(3.19)

where we discarded the irrelevant constant factor
C_{\alpha}
. Let us carefully examine the second contribution, splitting the analysis into two steps.

Step 2a
. First and foremost, we need to devise a suitable estimate for the zeroth seminorm of the functional derivatives of
\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}
. This will entail that the multi-linear operators
\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u}):F_{0}\times\ldots\times F_{0}\rightarrow F
are also bounded for all
p\in\mathbb{N}
with respect to the seminorm
||\cdot||_{0}
. Lemma
3.8
entails that, for any space compact smooth section
g\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{sc}({\mathcal{M}};\mathbb{C}^{2})
, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,p\}
, it holds true that

\displaystyle||(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{m_{i}}fg||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq\begin{cases}\widetilde{C}\sup_{\tau\leq t}||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}},\,\,\text{if}\,\,m_{i}=0\\
\widetilde{C}\exp{(C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2})}\sup_{\tau\leq t}||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}||v_{i}||_{4},\,\,\text{if}\,\,m_{i}=1\end{cases},

where
\widetilde{C}>0
is a suitable positive constant depending on
f
which incorporates all irrelevant numerical factors in Equations (
3.17
) and (
3.18
). It descends that, the worst possible scenario corresponds to
m_{i}=1
, that is

\displaystyle||\prod_{i=1}^{p}(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{m_{i}}fg||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq\widetilde{C}^{p}\exp{(pC^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2})}\sup_{\tau\leq t}||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}||v_{i}||_{4}\leq c\,\widetilde{C}^{p}\exp{(pC^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2})},

where
c>0
is a positive constant depending on
p
and on
\max_{i}||v_{i}||_{4}
. Similarly, we have

||(\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})g||^{t}_{H^{2}}\leq(1+||\widetilde{u}||_{2})\widetilde{C}\exp{(C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2})}\sup_{\tau\leq t}||g||^{\tau}_{H^{2}}\leq c^{\prime}\exp{(C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}||_{2})},

where we use that, per hypothesis,
||\widetilde{u}||_{2}\leq||u||_{2}+||u_{b}||_{2}\leq A^{\prime}
for some
A^{\prime}>0
. Now we introduce in Equation (
3.13
) a cutoff function
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathcal{M}})
such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a region containing the support of
f
. We need to use the above estimates on the integral kernel of
\widetilde{G}^{(}p)_{k,\ell}
:

a_{p}(x,y):=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left((\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{m_{i}}f\otimes(\mathbb{I}-\widetilde{u}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})(v_{i}^{(2)}\Delta^{\widetilde{u}}_{R})^{1-m_{i}}f\right)(\chi w\chi)(x,y)\in\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}).

It descends that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\text{supp}(f)}|a_{p}(x,x)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x,y\in\text{supp}(f)}|a_{p}(x,y)|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{t_{x},t_{y}\in\text{supp}(f)}(\widetilde{C}^{\prime})^{p}\exp{((p+2)||\widetilde{u}||_{2})}||\chi w\chi||^{(t_{x},t_{y})}_{H^{2}\times H^{2}},

where
||\cdot||^{(t_{x},t_{y})}_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}
denotes the Sobolev norm on
\Sigma_{t_{x}}\times\Sigma_{t_{y}}
and we exploit the fact that, for linear operators
A_{1}:H^{2}(\Sigma_{t_{x}})\rightarrow H^{2}(\Sigma_{t_{x}}),A_{2}:H^{2}(\Sigma_{t_{y}})\rightarrow H^{2}(\Sigma_{t_{y}})
and for any smooth function
W\in C^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t_{x}}\times\Sigma_{t_{y}})
, it holds that

||(A_{1}\otimes A_{2})W||^{(t_{x},t_{y})}_{H^{2}\times H^{2}}\leq||A_{1}||^{t_{x}}_{H^{2}_{x}}||A_{2}||^{t_{y}}_{H^{2}_{y}}||W||^{(t_{x},t_{y})}_{H^{2}\times H^{2}},

with
||\cdot||^{t_{i}}_{H^{2}_{i}}
denoting the corresponding operator norms. Since
e^{C^{\prime}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{2}}\leq C_{1}(1+||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{2})
for a sufficiently large positive constant
C_{1}>0
, we can conclude that, for fixed
p\in\{1,...,m\}

||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}\leq\text{const}.(1+||\widetilde{u}||_{2}),

(3.20)

where
const.
is independent on
\widetilde{u}
.

Step 2b
. Going back to Equation (
3.19
), an argument analogous to the one discussed in
[
DP23
, Lem. 4.1]
implies that we can estimate

||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||^{p}_{m-p+1}<C||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||_{0}^{p-1}||\widetilde{u}||_{m+3},

hence,

\displaystyle||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{n}

\displaystyle<C\left(||\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{p=1}^{m}||\widetilde{G}^{(p)}_{k,\ell}(\widetilde{u})||_{0}||\widetilde{u}^{(2)}||^{p-1}_{0}||\widetilde{u}||_{m+3}\right)

\displaystyle<C(1+||\widetilde{u}||_{2})\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{p=1}^{m}||\widetilde{u}||^{m-1}_{2}||\widetilde{u}||_{m+3}\right)

\displaystyle<C(1+||\widetilde{u}||_{n+3}),

where in the second line we use that
||\widetilde{u}||_{m+3}\leq||\widetilde{u}||_{n+3}
and with a slight abuse of notation we still denote by
C
the overall constant which depends on
n
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Hence, since the RG operator is built as a linear combination of tame smooth functionals, we obtain the sought result.

Proposition 3.10
.

Denote by
\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}
a small neighborhood of
0
and assume that, for any
u\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists some positive constant
A
such that
||u||_{2}\leq A
. Then the RG operator
\mathcal{RG}:\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}\rightarrow F
as per Equation (
3.16
) is tame smooth – see Appendix
A
.

Step 2: The linearized RG operator is tame smooth
. The goal is to prove the validity of item
1.
in Theorem
3.7
. Adopting the same notations as in Step 1, we define the linearized RG operator as the linear map

L:(u_{b}+\mathcal{U})\times F_{0}\rightarrow F,\,\,L(u)v:=\partial_{k}v-\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}[[\sigma(u)](v)],

(3.21)

where

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}[[\sigma(u)](v)]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)=\frac{1}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1}\in\{0,1\}}\left[(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})(v^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})^{m_{1}}f\otimes(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})(v^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})^{1-m_{1}}f(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}(x,x),

where
[\cdot]_{\ell\ell}(y,x)
is as per Notation
3.5
. Observe that, since
\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}
is constant in
k
, the dependence of
\sigma_{\ell}(u)(\cdot)
on
(\Phi,k)
is only through
u^{(2)}
.

Remark 3.11
.

In contrast to the case of a single scalar field, here
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
is
not
a multiplicative operator acting on
v^{(2)}
. A direct inspection entails that it is not possible to factor out the dependence on the second field derivatives of
v
and, hence,
\sigma_{\ell}(u)(\cdot)
should be thought of as a linear operator acting on
v^{(2)}
.

Remark 3.12
.

In our setting, the diffusion operator appearing in the linearized RG equation is no longer a scalar but it becomes a
2
by
2
matrix acting on the second partial derivatives of
v\in F_{0}
with respect to the fields. Thus, we can rewrite the action of
L(u)
making the matrix components explicit as follows. Define

\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v):=\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}[[\sigma(u)](v)]=\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}[\sigma(u)](v)(x,x).

Hence, we can define an integral operator
\Sigma(u)(\cdot):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}[\sigma(u)](\cdot)(x,x)
acting on
v^{(2)}
such that

\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}[[\sigma(u)](v)]:=\sum_{i,j=1,2}\Sigma_{ij}(u)(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}v),

with components

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ij}(u)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\sum_{n,m=1,2}\left((\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})_{\ell i}(\Delta^{u}_{R})_{jn}(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})_{\ell m}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})_{\ell n}(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R})_{\ell i}(\Delta^{u}_{R})_{jm}\right)(w)_{nm}(x,x).

(3.22)

In order to prove that the linearized RG operator
L
is a tame smooth map, we shall need the following property of the linear operators
\sigma_{\ell}
.

Proposition 3.13
.

The linear maps
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
are tame smooth for each
\ell=1,2
.

Proof.

Note that, by direct inspection,
[\sigma^{(n)}_{\ell}(u)](v_{1},...,v_{n})=\widetilde{G}^{(n+1)}_{k,\ell}(u;v_{1},...,v_{n})
and, thus,
[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
is smooth as a function of
u^{(2)}
. In addition, being related to the functional derivative of
\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}
, on account of Lemma
3.9
it is straightforward to conclude that
\sigma_{\ell}(u):F_{0}\rightarrow F
is also a tame map. We conclude by observing that the linear combination of tame smooth maps
\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
is also tame smooth.
∎

Especially in view of the physical applications, it seems natural to wonder whether, with a suitable choice of states, one can guarantee that for each
\ell=1,2
,
[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
is strictly positive as well as
||u||_{2}\leq A
in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
0
. The answer is affirmative as illustrated by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.14
.

Consider the initial, boundary-value problem in Equation (
3.12
). Assume that the functions
\beta,\psi
and
u_{b}\in F
are chosen in such a way that there exists
\epsilon>0
for which
||\beta||_{2}+||\psi||_{2}<\epsilon
and
||u_{b}||_{2}\leq\epsilon
. Then we can always choose
w\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\text{Mat}(2;\mathbb{C}))
for which there exists
\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}
, such that, for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
v\in F_{0}

[\sigma_{\ell}(\widetilde{u})](v):=[\sigma_{\ell}(u_{b}+u)](v)\geq c>0
and
||u||_{2}<A=\epsilon
.

Proof.

We fix
\ell=1,2
and observe that, on account of Proposition
3.13
, we can write

[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)=[\sigma_{\ell}(0)](v)+\int_{0}^{1}d\lambda\,\frac{d}{d\lambda}[\sigma_{\ell}(\lambda(u+u_{b}))](v)\geq[\sigma_{\ell}(0)](v)-\sup_{\lambda\in[0,1]}||[\sigma^{(1)}_{\ell}(\lambda\widetilde{u})](v)(u+u_{b})||_{0},

(3.23)

where we denote by
\sigma_{\ell}^{(1)}
the functional derivative of
[\sigma_{\ell}(\widetilde{u})](v)
with respect to
\widetilde{u}
.
Since

[\sigma_{\ell}(0)](v):=\frac{1}{2||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}^{2}}d\mu_{x}d\mu_{y}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\ell}(x)\sum_{m\in\{0,1\}}\left[((v^{(2)}\Delta_{R})^{m}f\otimes(v^{(2)}\Delta_{R})^{1-m}f)(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}(y,x),

is linear in
w
and it cannot be identically zero for any
w
, we can choose a state such that
[\sigma_{\ell}(0)](v)\geq(2\epsilon C+c)>0
, where
C>\sup_{\lambda}||\sigma^{(1)}_{\ell}(\lambda\widetilde{u})||_{0}
. Thanks to the smoothness of
\sigma_{\ell}(\widetilde{u})
, we can choose
u_{b}
such that
||u_{b}||_{2}\leq(||\beta||_{2}+||\psi||_{2})<\epsilon
and consider a sufficiently small neighborhood of
0
,
\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}
such that
||u||_{2}<\epsilon,\forall u\in\mathcal{U}
. Furthermore, since
\sigma_{\ell}^{(1)}(\widetilde{u})(v,v_{1})=\widetilde{G}_{k,\ell}^{(2)}(v,v_{1})
, upon taking the supremum norm over
v,v_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle||[\sigma^{(1)}(\lambda\widetilde{u})](u+u_{b})||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||\sigma^{(1)}(\lambda\widetilde{u})||_{0}||u_{b}+u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq||\widetilde{G}^{(2)}_{k,\ell}(\lambda\widetilde{u})||_{0}||u_{b}+u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}(1+||u+u_{b}||_{2})||u_{b}+u||_{0}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}||u_{b}+u||_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\epsilon C^{\prime\prime},

for suitable constants
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
which depends on
\epsilon
.
∎

In
Step 4
, in order to prove that the inverse of the linearized RG operator is a tame smooth map, we shall make an additional requirement on the diffusion coefficient, which is justified in view of the following proposition.

Proposition 3.15
.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
and choose
u_{b}\in F
compatible with the boundary conditions such that
||u_{b}||_{3}\leq A
, with
A>0
sufficiently small. If
[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
is such that
(b-a)
is sufficiently small, it holds that, for all
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
||u||_{4}<A
,

||K(Du)||_{0}:=\sup_{||v||_{2}=1}||[K(Du)](v)||_{0}<\epsilon^{\prime},

(3.24)

where
D\in\{\partial_{\varphi_{1}},\partial_{\varphi_{2}},\partial_{k}\}
and

[K(Du)](v):=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[D(\sigma_{\ell}(u))](v)}{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)},

for any
v\in F_{0}
.

Proof.

On account of Proposition
3.14
, we have that
\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)\geq c>0
for some constant
c
. This implies that

\frac{1}{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)}<\frac{1}{c},\,\,\,v\in F_{0}.

Note that for each
\ell=1,2
, we have that

[D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,k))](v)=[D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,a))](v)+\int_{a}^{k}\partial_{\chi}[D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,\chi))](v)\,d\chi.

The smoothness of both
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
and its first functional derivative entail that

||D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,k))||_{0}\leq||D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,a))||_{0}+(b-a)||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{2}.

Exploiting the fact that
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
are also tame maps, we have
||\sigma_{\ell}||_{2}\leq(1+||u||_{4})\leq C(1+A)
. Additionally, the continuity of
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
with respect to
u
entails that

|D(\sigma_{\ell}(u(\Phi,a))(v)|\leq C||D[u_{b}^{(2)}(\Phi,a)||_{\infty}<||u_{b}||_{3}\leq A,

and, thus,

||K(Du)||_{0}<\frac{C}{c}(A+(b-a)(1+A)\leq\epsilon^{\prime},

for sufficiently small
A
and
b-a
.
∎

We can now prove the following proposition.

Proposition 3.16
.

The linearization of the RG operator
L(u)v=\partial_{k}v-\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
is tame smooth.

Proof.

Via a combination of Leibnitz rule and an interpolating argument one obtains that

\displaystyle||L(u)v||_{n}

\displaystyle\leq||v||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)||_{n}

\displaystyle\leq||v||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n}||v||_{n+2}

for some positive constant
C
. Since
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
is tame smooth for each
\ell=1,2
, see Proposition
3.13
, and
L(u)
is a linear combination of tame maps,
L:\mathcal{U}\times F_{0}\rightarrow F
is tame smooth.
∎

Step 3: The linearization of the RG operator is invertible
. Under the hypothesis that
\sigma_{\ell}>0
on
X\times[a,b]
, the linearized RG operator
L(u):F_{0}\rightarrow F
is a parabolic operator.

We have the following result – see
[
DP23
]
.

Proposition 3.17

(Existence and uniqueness of an inverse)
.

Let
u\in\mathcal{U}
and denote by
L:=L(u)
the linearized RG operator as per Equation (
3.21
). Under the assumption that
[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
,
\ell=1,2
, is strictly positive for every
u\in\mathcal{U}
,
v\in F_{0}
, there exists a unique inverse
E:F\rightarrow F_{0}
compatible with the initial and boundary conditions
E(g)(\Phi,a)=0
,
E(g)|_{\partial X\times[a,b]}=0
, for all
g\in C^{\infty}_{0}(X\times[a,b])
, such that

E(Lg)=L(E(g))=g,\,\,\,g\in F_{0}.

Furthermore, the inverse is continuous in the following sense:

||E(g)||_{0}<C||g||_{0},\,\,\text{for some positive constant}\,\,C>0.

Step 4: The inverse is tame smooth
. Having assessed existence and uniqueness of the inverse, we need to show that the map
E
is tame smooth, so to apply the Nash-Moser theorem in Hamilton formulation. To this end, we shall need two auxiliary results.

Lemma 3.18
.

Assume that for each
\ell=1,2

[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
is strictly positive for
v\in F_{0}
and
u\in\mathcal{U}\subset F_{0}
such that
||u||_{4}\leq A
. In addition, suppose that there exists a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
for which
\sup_{D}\sup_{||v||_{2}=1}||[K(Du)](v)||_{0}<\epsilon
, where
[K(Du)](v):=\frac{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[D(\sigma_{\ell}(u))](v)}{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)}
for
v\in F_{0}
, while
D\in\{\partial_{\varphi_{1}},\partial_{\varphi_{2}},\partial_{k}\}
, see Proposition
3.15
. Then, the following estimate holds true:

||v||_{1}<C(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}),

(3.25)

for some positive constant
C>0
.

Proof.

Recall that the linearized RG operator is given by
L(u)v=\partial_{k}v-\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
. The uniform continuity of the inverse
E
entails that if
L(u)v=g
, then

||E(g)||_{0}=||v||_{0}<C||g||_{0}.

Consider now
D\in\{\partial_{\varphi_{1}},\partial_{\varphi_{2}},\partial_{k}\}
and apply the above estimate to
Dv
:

||Dv||_{0}<C||L(u)Dv||_{0}.

Note that

L(u)Dv=\partial_{k}Dv-\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](Dv)=D(L(u)v)-\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}^{(1)}(u)(Du)](v),

where we exploit the commutativity of
D
with the partial derivatives,
i.e.
,
(Dv)^{(2)}=Dv^{(2)}
, and we denote by
[\sigma_{\ell}^{(1)}(u)]
the functional derivative of the linear operator
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
as a function of
u
. In particular, being
[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)
strictly positive by hypothesis, one can multiply and divide by
\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v):=-L(u)v+\partial_{k}v
, thus obtaining

\displaystyle L(u)Dv

\displaystyle=D(L(u)v)+\frac{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}^{(1)}(u)(Du)](v)}{\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)}(L(u)v-\partial_{k}v).

\displaystyle=D(L(u)v)+[K(Du)](v)(L(u)v-\partial_{k}v).

Therefore, the uniform continuity of
E
and the fact that
\sum_{\ell=1,2}[\sigma_{\ell}(u)](v)\geq c^{\prime}>0
imply that

\displaystyle||Dv||_{0}

\displaystyle<C(||D(L(u)v)||_{0}+||K(Du)||_{0}(||L(u)v||_{0}+||v||_{1}))

\displaystyle<C(||Dg||_{0}+||K(Du)||_{0}(||g||_{0}+||v||_{1})).

Thus, using the fact that
||u||_{1}\leq||u||_{4}\leq A
, we have that

\displaystyle||v||_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(||v||_{0}+\sum_{D\in\{\partial_{\varphi_{1}},\partial_{\varphi_{2}},\partial_{k}\}}||Dv||_{0}\right)

\displaystyle<C(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}+\sup_{D}||K(Du)||_{0}||v||_{1}),

which entails that

\displaystyle(1-C\sup_{D}||K(Du)||_{0})||v||_{1}<C(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}|g||_{0}).

Since, by hypothesis,
\sup_{D}||K(Du)||_{0}<\epsilon
, for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, we can guarantee that
(1-C\sup_{D}||K(Du)||_{0})>0
and, hence, the sought result descends:

||v||_{1}<C^{\prime}(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}),

for a suitable positive constant
C^{\prime}>0
.
∎

Lemma 3.19
.

In the same setting of Lemma
3.18
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

||v||_{n}<C(||g||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}),

(3.26)

for some positive constant
C>0
.

Proof.

The proof goes by induction with respect to
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The case
n=1
is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.18
and the standard property that
||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}\leq||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{2}
– which still holds true at level of operator seminorms. Assume now that Equation (
3.26
) is valid up to
n-1
. We shall prove that it holds true also for
n
. To this avail, apply Equation (
3.26
) to
Dv
with
D\in\{\partial_{\varphi_{1}},\partial_{\varphi_{2}},\partial_{k}\}
:

||Dv||_{n-1}<C(||L(u)Dv||_{n}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n}||L(u)Dv||_{0}),

where we used that
L(u)v=g
. We need to estimate separately the two contributions.

\displaystyle||L(u)Dv||_{n}

\displaystyle<||g||_{n+1}+C(||[K(Du)](v)g||_{n}+||[K(Du)](v)\partial_{k}v||_{n})

\displaystyle<||g||_{n+1}+C\left(||K(Du)||_{0}||g||_{n}+||K(Du)||_{0}||\partial_{k}v||_{n}\right)

\displaystyle<||g||_{n+1}+C\left(\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{n}+||K(Du)||_{0}||\partial_{k}v||_{n}\right),

where
[K(Du)](v)
is as above. By an interpolation argument, we can write

\displaystyle||L(u)Dv||_{n}

\displaystyle<||g||_{n+1}+C\sum_{\ell=1,2}\left(||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{0}||g||_{n+1}+||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+||K(Du)||_{0}||\partial_{k}v||_{n}+||K(Du)||_{n}||\partial_{k}v||_{0}\right)

\displaystyle<C\left((1+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{0})||g||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+||K(Du)||_{0}||\partial_{k}v||_{n}+||K(Du)||_{n}||v||_{1}\right).

Using now Lemma
3.18
, we can conclude that, modulus constant factors,

\displaystyle||L(u)Dv||_{n}

\displaystyle<(1+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{0})||g||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}

\displaystyle+||K(Du)||_{0}||v||_{n+1}+||K(Du)||_{n}(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}).

In addition, Lemma
3.18
together with the Leibnitz rule entail that

\displaystyle||L(u)Dv||_{0}

\displaystyle<||g||_{1}+||K(Du)||_{0}(||g||_{0}+||v||_{1})

\displaystyle<||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}((1+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1})||g||_{0}+||g||_{1}).

Hence, combining the two inequalities we get

\displaystyle(1-C||K(Du)||_{0})||v||_{n}

\displaystyle<C\left[(1+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{0})||g||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+||K(Du)||_{n}(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0})\right.+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n}

\displaystyle\left.\left(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}(||g||_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}+||g||_{1})\right)\right].

Since we have that
(1-C||K(Du)||_{0})>0
, we can divide both sides of the inequality by this quantity, thus obtaining

\displaystyle||v||_{n}

\displaystyle<C\left[(1+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{0})||g||_{n+1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}||g||_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+||K(Du)||_{n}(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0})\right.+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n}

\displaystyle\left.\left(||g||_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}(||g||_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}||g||_{0}+||g||_{1})\right)\right].

Invoking an interpolating argument, we can write

||g||_{1}||h||_{n}\leq C(||g||_{0}||h||_{n+1}+||g||_{n+1}||h||_{0}).

Furthermore, we have that
\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{1}<A
and, hence,
||K(Du)||_{n}\leq C\sum_{\ell=1,2}||\sigma_{\ell}(u)||_{n+1}
. The sought result thus descends.
∎

Proposition 3.20
.

Assume that
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
is strictly positive for each
\ell=1,2
and
u\in\mathcal{U}
such that
||u||_{4}\leq A
for some positive constant
A
. In addition, suppose that
\sup_{D}||K(Du)||_{0}<\epsilon
, where
K(Du)(v)
is as per Lemma
3.18
. Then, the inverse
E
is tame smooth.

Proof.

The inverse
E
being tame smooth means that, there exists a positive constant
C>0
, such that

||E(g)||_{n}\leq C(1+||g||_{n+r})

for suitable
r,n\in\mathbb{N}
. We begin by noticing that the dependence of
L(u)
on
u
is only through the linear operators
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
,
\ell=1,2
, which are tame smooth maps of
u
. Since the composition of tame smooth maps is tame, it suffices to study how
L(u)
depends on
\sigma_{\ell}(u)
,
\ell=1,2
. More precisely, Lemmata
3.18
and
3.19
provide estimates which allows us to control the seminorms of the derivative of
v
with those of
g
. Hence, the desired result is a direct consequence of these two.
∎

Thanks to the estimates in the previous proposition, a direct application of the Nash-Moser theorem
3.7
entails the following.

Theorem 3.21
.

The RG operator admits a unique family of tame smooth local inverses and unique local solutions exist.

3.1.1
Stochastic fields in the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism

Via the Martin-Siggia-Rose formalism, this case can be regarded as a specific instance of the two, mutually interacting scalar fields with the caveats discussed in Section
2.1.1
. Indeed, working in the Lorentzian setting, the MSR fields
\varphi,\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and, thus, we can introduce the field doublet

\bm{\Phi}:=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi\\
\widetilde{\varphi}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The analysis of this scenario proceeds along the same lines as in the two-scalar-field case. Yet, a crucial distinction arises in the interpretation of the results, since
\widetilde{\varphi}
plays the rôle of an
auxiliary
field associated with the stochastic contribution to the equation of motion.

In this case, the fields are smooth sections of the trivial vector bundle
E:=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and we can set
\mathcal{E}:=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
. As the mass term of the theory is proportional to the product
\varphi\widetilde{\varphi}
, it is convenient to choose an infrared cut off of the form:

Q_{k}(\chi,\widetilde{\chi})=-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,q_{k}(x)T(\chi(x)\widetilde{\chi}(x))=-\frac{1}{2}\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}[\mathfrak{q}_{k}T(\bm{\chi}\otimes\widetilde{\bm{\chi}})],

(3.27)

where
\mathfrak{q}_{k}:=q_{k}\mathbb{I}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}
and the factor
\frac{1}{2}
takes into account the commutativity of the fields – see Remark
2.32
. The analysis then closely mimics that of Section
3.1
, hence we refrain from repeating the discussion.

3.2
(B) Self-interacting Dirac fields

The last scenario we examine is that of a self-interacting Dirac field on a
d-
dimensional globally hyperbolic spacetime
{\mathcal{M}}
, see Section
2.2
. In this context, the rôle of
E
is played by the vector bundle
\mathcal{X}:=D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}}
, where
D{\mathcal{M}}
and
D^{*}{\mathcal{M}}
are respectively the Dirac bundle and its dual. Assuming triviality of the underlying spin bundle,
D{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{\mathcal{M}}\times\Sigma_{d}
and
D^{*}{\mathcal{M}}\simeq{\mathcal{M}}\times\Sigma_{d}^{*}
, where
\Sigma_{d}:=\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}
with
N_{d}:=2^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor}
is the
spinor space
. Also in this case we can introduce a field doublet

\Psi:=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\rho}\\
\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}},

(3.28)

where
\psi^{\rho}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\Sigma_{d})
,
\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}};\Sigma^{*}_{d})
and we exploited the fact that
\Sigma_{d}
and
\Sigma_{d}^{*}
are canonically isomorphic as complex vector spaces. A few remarks are in due order:

❈

Observe that Equation (
3.28
) involves objects with a different Dirac index structure. To avoid this potential hurdle, one may alternatively choose the field doublet in the
Nambu–Gorkov
basis,
i.e.
,

\Psi_{NG}:=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\rho}\\
(\bar{\psi}^{T})^{\rho}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D{\mathcal{M}})\simeq C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}}.

The advantage of such a choice of basis lies in the fact that it allows us to write the relevant matrix-valued operators as

A=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&A_{12}\\
A_{21}&A_{22}\end{pmatrix}\in\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{2N_{d}}),

where each entry is an endomorphism of
\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}
. However, as the Wetterich equation entails a trace over the Dirac indices,
cf.
, Equation (
3.2
), this does not present an obstruction, and we therefore retain our original choice.

❈

Notice, in addition, that the field doublet mixes two types of indices: the
\mathbb{C}^{2}
-vector indices of the field doublet and the Dirac indices of its spinorial components, here denoted by Greek letters. The distinct nature of the indices does not constitute a problem for the ensuing discussion, yet it is convenient to keep in mind that they carry an intrinsically different meaning.

Notation 3.22
.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{E}:=\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
and let
A:\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
be a matrix-valued operator which in block-form reads:

A:=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&A_{12}\\
A_{21}&A_{22}\end{pmatrix},

where
A_{11}:=(A_{11})^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}:\Gamma(D{\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\Gamma(D{\mathcal{M}})
,
A_{12}:=(A_{12})^{\rho\rho^{\prime}}:\Gamma(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\Gamma(D{\mathcal{M}})
,
A_{21}:=(A_{21})_{\rho\rho^{\prime}}:\Gamma(D{\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\Gamma(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
and
A_{22}:=(A_{22})_{\rho^{\prime}}{}^{\rho}:\Gamma(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})\rightarrow\Gamma(D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
. Hence, we can formally define its Hilbert space trace on
\mathcal{E}
as:

\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}(A):=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\text{tr}_{\text{Dirac}}[\text{tr}_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}A](x,x)=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\sum_{\ell=1,2}\sum_{\rho=1}^{N_{d}}(A_{\ell\ell})^{\rho}{}_{\rho}(x,x).

(3.29)

Notice that the Dirac trace
\text{tr}_{\text{Dirac}}
can only be defined for endomorphism of
D{\mathcal{M}}
or
D^{*}{\mathcal{M}}
.
This suggests that, since the final result should be a scalar also in the Dirac indices, the off-diagonal elements should not play any rôle in the derivation of the Renormalization Group flow equations.

Henceforth, let us consider an infrared mass regulator of the form

\displaystyle Q_{k}(\eta,\bar{\eta})

\displaystyle:=-\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\left[\mathfrak{q}_{k}T(\bm{\eta}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\overline{\bm{\eta}})\right]

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[T(\eta^{\rho}[q_{k,\psi}]_{\rho}{}^{\rho^{\prime}}\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}})(x)-T(\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}}[q_{k,\bar{\psi}}]^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}\eta^{\rho})(x)\right],

(3.30)

where we denote by

\mathsf{q}_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}q_{k,\psi}&0\\
0&-q_{k,\bar{\psi}}\end{pmatrix},\quad(\mathfrak{q}_{k})_{11}:=q_{k,\psi}\,\text{and}\,(\mathfrak{q}_{k})_{22}:=-q_{k,\bar{\psi}},

while
\bm{\eta}\in\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
is the field doublet as per Equation (
3.28
). In order to further simplify the ensuing analysis, we shall assume that each non-vanishing entry of
\mathfrak{q}_{k}
is proportional to the identity in the Dirac indices and it depends linearly on
k
, that is:

(q_{k,\psi})^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x):=\epsilon kf(x)\mathbb{I}^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}},\,\,\epsilon>0

(3.31)

and, analogously for
q_{k,\bar{\psi}}
, where
f
is the same adiabatic cut off that will be chosen in the LPA. Without loss of generality, we shall set
\epsilon\equiv 1
and assume that
k\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We highlight that any contribution to Equation (
3.31
) constant in
k
can be incorporated into the free mass of the theory.
Let

\displaystyle J(\eta,\bar{\eta})

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\overline{\bm{j}}^{T}(x)\bm{\eta}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\left[\bar{j}_{\rho}(x)\eta^{\rho}(x)+j^{\rho}(x)\bar{\eta}_{\rho}(x)\right],\,\,\bm{j}=\begin{pmatrix}j\\
\bar{j}\end{pmatrix}\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{X})

(3.32)

be an external current and

\mathrm{P}_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}P_{0,\psi}&0\\
0&P_{0,\bar{\psi}}\end{pmatrix},

(3.33)

be the operator implementing the free equations of motion, where
P_{0,\psi}\equiv(P_{0,\psi})^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}:=\not{D}^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}
,
P_{0,\bar{\psi}}\equiv(P_{0,\bar{\psi}})_{\rho}{}^{\rho^{\prime}}:=(\not{D}^{*})_{\rho}{}^{\rho^{\prime}}
. Furthermore, we define by

Z_{k}(j,\bar{j})=\omega(S(V)^{-1}\star S(V+Q_{k}+J)),\quad W_{k}(j,\bar{j})=-i\log Z_{k}(j,\bar{j}),

(3.34)

the generating functional of the regularised theory and its connected counterpart, respectively.
By direct computation the Hessian of
W_{k}(j,\bar{j})
reads in a convenient choice of basis:

[W_{k}^{(2)}(j,\bar{j})](x,y)=i\begin{pmatrix}\langle\eta(x)\cdot_{T}\bar{\eta}(y)\rangle-\psi(x)\bar{\psi}(y)&\underbrace{\langle\eta(x)\eta(y)\rangle}_{0}-\psi(x)\psi(y)\\
\underbrace{\langle\bar{\eta}(x)\bar{\eta}(y)}_{0}\rangle-\bar{\psi}(x)\bar{\psi}(y)&\langle\bar{\eta}(x)\cdot_{T}\eta(y)\rangle-\bar{\psi}(x)\psi(y)\end{pmatrix},

(3.35)

where
\psi,\bar{\psi}
are the classical fields,
i.e.
,
\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta j(x)}=\langle\bar{\eta}(x)\rangle=:\bar{\psi}(x)
and
\frac{\delta W_{k}}{\delta\bar{j}(x)}=\langle\eta(x)\rangle=:\psi(x)
, and, for the sake of notational ease, we have left the Dirac indices understood. Note that the off-diagonal correlations
\langle\eta(x)\eta(y)\rangle
and
\langle\bar{\eta}(x)\bar{\eta}(y)\rangle
must be identically zero since such terms break the Fermionic number symmetry. As a consequence, the
Polchinski equation
involves only the diagonal contributions in Equation (
3.35
) and it reads:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}W_{k}(j,\bar{j})

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{k}Q_{k}(\eta,\bar{\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[T(\eta^{\rho}[\partial_{k}q_{k,\psi}]_{\rho}{}^{\rho^{\prime}}\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}})(x)-T(\bar{\eta}_{\rho^{\prime}}[\partial_{k}q_{k,\bar{\psi}}]^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}\eta^{\rho})(x)\right]

\displaystyle=-\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\left((\partial_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k})^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}\left[-iW_{k}^{(2)}+\Psi\otimes_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\bar{\Psi}\right]^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}\right),

where
\langle\cdot\rangle
denotes the weighted expectation value. Observe that the above equation is only formal, as a regularization procedure needs to be implemented for the time ordered products to yield a finite result. Thereby, denoting by
G_{k,\psi}:=\langle\eta\cdot_{T}\bar{\eta}\rangle
and
G_{k,\bar{\psi}}:=\langle\bar{\eta}\cdot_{T}\eta\rangle
, the
Renormalization Group (RG) flow equation
reads:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}\Gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{k}Q_{k}\rangle-\partial_{k}Q_{k}

\displaystyle=-\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\partial_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}:[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}-\mathfrak{q}_{k}]^{-1}:_{\widetilde{\mathrm{H}}_{F}}\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left[\partial_{k}(q_{k,\psi})^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}(x):(G_{k,\psi})^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x,x):_{\widetilde{H}_{F,\psi}}-\partial_{k}(q_{k,\bar{\psi}})^{\rho^{\prime}}{}_{\rho}(x):(G_{k,\bar{\psi}})^{\rho}{}_{\rho^{\prime}}(x,x):_{\widetilde{H}_{F,\bar{\psi}}}\right],

(3.36)

where
\Gamma_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi}):=W_{k}(\bar{j}_{\psi},j_{\bar{\psi}})-J(\psi,\bar{\psi})-Q_{k}(\psi,\bar{\psi})
is the average effective action, whilst
\widetilde{H}_{F,\psi/\bar{\psi}}
are the counterterms arising from the point splitting procedure. The
effective potential

U_{k}
is then implicitly defined via the following relation:

\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}(\psi,\bar{\psi})-\mathfrak{q}_{k}:=\mathrm{P}_{0}+U_{k}^{(2)}(\psi,\bar{\psi})=:\mathrm{P}_{k},

where the Dirac indices are left understood for notational brevity. In terms of the
\mathbb{C}^{2}-
matrix components, we can write:

\begin{cases}[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{11}-q_{k,\psi}=:P_{0,\psi}+[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{11},\quad[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{22}+q_{k,\bar{\psi}}=:P_{0,\bar{\psi}}+[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{22}\\
[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{12}=:[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{12}\quad[\Gamma_{k}^{(2)}]_{21}=:[U_{k}^{(2)}]_{21}.\end{cases}

Notation 3.23
.

Not to excessively burden the notation, we will refrain from indicating explicitly the Dirac indices, whenever we feel that confusion may not arise.

In the local potential approximation, see Section
3.1
, the quantum wave operator
\mathrm{P}_{k}
is still Green hyperbolic and, thus, it admits unique advanced and retarded Green operators:

\displaystyle\Delta^{U}_{A/R}:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\Gamma^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}_{k}\circ\Delta^{U}_{A/R}=\Delta^{U}_{A/R}\circ\mathrm{P}_{k}

\displaystyle=\text{id}|_{\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{X})},\quad\text{supp}\Delta^{U}_{A/R}(\Psi)\subseteq J^{\mp}(\text{supp}(\Psi)),

for all
\Psi\in\Gamma^{\infty}_{0}(\mathcal{X})
. With a slight abuse of notation, we will still indicate by
\Delta^{U}_{A/R}
the associated matrix-valued bi-distributions in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\mathcal{X}\boxtimes\mathcal{X})
,
i.e.
,

\Delta^{U}_{A/R}=\begin{pmatrix}[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{11}&[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{12}\\
[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{21}&[\Delta^{U}_{A/R}]_{22}\end{pmatrix},

(3.37)

where each matrix entry is a bi-distribution lying in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};D{\mathcal{M}}\oplus D^{*}{\mathcal{M}})
. By means of the advanced and retarded quantum Møller operators, we can rewrite Equation (
3.2
) in terms of
U_{k}
as follows:

\displaystyle\partial_{k}U_{k}

\displaystyle=-\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\left(\partial_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}[(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})w(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{A})]\right)

\displaystyle-\int_{{\mathcal{M}}}d\mu_{x}\,\partial_{k}q_{k,\psi}[(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})w(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{A})]_{11}(x,x)-\partial_{k}q_{k,\bar{\psi}}[(\mathbb{I}-\Delta^{U}_{R}U_{k}^{(2)})w(\mathbb{I}-U_{k}^{(2)}\Delta^{U}_{A})]_{22},

(3.38)

where
w\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\mathcal{X}\boxtimes\mathcal{X})
reads:

w=\begin{pmatrix}w_{\psi}&0\\
0&w_{\bar{\psi}},\end{pmatrix}

where
w_{\psi}(x,y):=\Delta_{F,\psi}(x,y)-H_{F,\psi}(x,y)
and
w_{\bar{\psi}}(x,y):=\Delta_{F,\bar{\psi}}(x,y)-H_{F,\bar{\psi}}(x,y)
.

Under the same assumptions on the effective potential detailed in Section
3.1
, Equation (
3.2
) can be equivalently formulated as a mixed Cauchy problem for its integral kernel
u(\Psi,k)
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{k}u=\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}(\mathrm{G}_{k}(u^{(2)}))\\
u(\Psi,a)=\alpha\\
u|_{\partial X\times[a,b]}=\beta\end{cases}

(3.39)

where
X\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a compact set encompassing all possible values of the field doublet
\Psi
,
k\in[a,b]\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, whilst
\alpha\in C^{\infty}(X)
and
\beta\in C^{\infty}(\partial X\times[a,b])
are assigned functions. Here
u^{(2)}
is the Hessian matrix of
u
, which in a convenient choice of basis reads:

u^{(2)}(\Psi,k):=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial\Psi}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\Psi}}\right)u(\Psi,k)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\psi}\partial_{\bar{\psi}}u&\partial_{\psi}\partial_{\psi}u\\
\partial_{\bar{\psi}}\partial_{\bar{\psi}}u&\partial_{\bar{\psi}}\partial_{{\psi}}u\end{pmatrix}.

In the above equation, the functional
G_{k}(u^{(2)}):=\sum_{\ell=1,2}G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)})
, is defined as follows:

G_{k,\ell}(u^{(2)}):=-\frac{1}{||f||_{L^{1}}}\int_{\mathcal{M}}d\mu_{x}\,\left((\partial_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k})_{\ell\ell}(x)\left[(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R}f)\otimes(\mathbb{I}-u^{(2)}\Delta^{u}_{R}f)(w)\right]_{\ell\ell}\right)^{\rho}{}_{\rho}(x,x),

(3.40)

where
\Delta^{u}_{R}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{M}};\mathcal{X}\boxtimes\mathcal{X})
is the retarded fundamental solution of the Green hyperbolic operator
\mathrm{P}_{0}+fu^{(2)}
. Barring the occurrence of Dirac indices, the analysis is then identical to the one in Section
3.1
, hence we omit it.

Remark 3.24
.

All the operational seminorms should be taken both with respect to the Dirac indices and to the fictitious
\mathbb{C}^{2}
indices corresponding to the components of the field doublet. If we assume the underlying spin bundle to be trivial, this poses no further complications in the analysis, as both the Dirac indices in
\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2}
and the
\mathbb{C}^{2}-
matrix ones can be treated on equal footing. As a consequence, all the estimates derived in Section
3.1
for the two scalar fields are also true in the present setting. For instance, taking
u\in C^{\infty}(X\times[a,b])
, the associated Hessian matrix
u^{(2)}\in\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
. Therefore, we can set

||u^{(2)}||_{0}:=\sup_{\Psi,k}|u^{(2)}(\Psi,k)|,

where
|\cdot|
denotes the matrix norm on
\text{End}(\mathbb{C}^{N_{d}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{2})
.

As a consequence, also in this case, we can establish the following result, whose proof is omitted, being identical to the one discussed in the scalar scenario.

Theorem 3.25
.

There exists a unique family of tame smooth local inverses to the RG operator for the Thirring model and, hence, unique local solutions exist.

Appendix A
Tame smooth maps

In this Appendix, we recall some relevant notions, useful to establish local existence of solutions to the RG flow equations via the Nash-Moser theorem – see Sections
3.1
,
3.1.1
and
3.2
. We refer the reader to
[
Mos66a
,
Mos66b
,
Nas56
]

[
Ham82
, Part II]
for a more comprehensive account of these topics.

Let
F
be a vector space. We call
seminorm
on
F
a function
||\cdot||:F\to\mathbb{R}
which satisfies the following requirements: for all
f,g\in F
and for all
c\in\mathbb{R}
,

1.

||f||\geq 0
,

2.

||f+g||\leq||f||+||g||
,

3.

||cf||=|c|||f||
.

Let
I
be an index set, possibly countable. The family of seminorms
\{||\cdot||_{i}\}_{i\in I}
uniquely a defines a topology: this is coarsest topology on
F
such that all seminorms are continuous, with a base of neighbourhoods of
0
given by finite intersections of sets of the form
\{f\in F\,|\,||f||_{i}<\epsilon\}
. More precisely, given a sequence of elements in
F
,
\{f_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset F
, and
f\in F
we say that
f_{j}
converges to
f
if
||f_{j}-f||_{i}\rightarrow 0
, for all
i\in I
. If
F
is endowed with such a collection of seminorm, we call
F
a
locally convex topological vector space
. The topology on
F
is said to be:

❈

Hausdorff
, if
||f||_{n}=0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
implies that
f=0
,

❈

metrizable
, if the family of seminorms
\{||\cdot||_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is countable.

In addition,
F
is
complete
is every Cauchy sequence in
F
is convergent, in the sense clarified above.

Definition A.1
.

A topological vector space
F
that is locally convex, Hausdorff, metrizable and complete is called
Fréchet space
. If the family of seminorms
\{||\cdot||_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is increasing in strength,
i.e.
,
||f||_{n}\leq||f||_{n+1}
, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
F
is said to be a
graded Fréchet space
.

Let
F
be a graded Fréchet space as per Definition
A.1
and let
B
be some Banach space. Denote by

\mathcal{S}_{\exp}(\mathbb{N},B):=\{(x_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset B\,|\,\exists\alpha>0\,\text{such that}\,\sup_{j}e^{\alpha j}||x_{j}||_{B}<\infty\},

the space of exponentially decreasing sequences on
B
.
F
is said to be
tame
if there exist two linear maps
L:F\to\mathcal{S}_{\exp}(\mathbb{N},B)
and
M:\mathcal{S}_{\exp}(\mathbb{N},B)\to F
such that
ML:F\to F
is the identity, namely,

F\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{L}}\mathcal{S}_{\exp}(\mathbb{N},B)\mathrel{\mathop{\longrightarrow}^{M}}F.

(A.1)

Therefore, we can formulate the following definition.

Definition A.2
.

Consider two graded Fréchet spaces
F
and
G
and let
\mathcal{U}\subset F
be an open subset of
F
. Suppose, additionally, that we have a map
P:\mathcal{U}\rightarrow G
. Then,
P
is
tame
of degree
r
and base
b
if it is continuous and if, for all
f
in a neighbourhood of
f_{0}\in\mathcal{U}
and for all
n\geq b
, there exists a constant
C\equiv C(n)>0
such that

||P(f)||_{n}\leq C(1+||f||_{n+r}).

(A.2)
